# Session log

Chronological record of what each Claude Code session built, decided, and learned on Platterpus — **newest first**. This is the project's institutional memory; it was split out of `CLAUDE.md` (2026-06-13) so the always-loaded project context stays lean. It *complements*, does not replace, the durable distillations:

- **Coding standards / critical rules** → [`CLAUDE.md`](../CLAUDE.md)
- **Architectural decisions** → [`PLANNING.md`](../PLANNING.md) (KDD-NN)
- **Engineering patterns & lessons** → [`architecture.md`](architecture.md), [`testing.md`](testing.md)
- **User-facing changes** → [`CHANGELOG.md`](../CHANGELOG.md)

**Graduation rule:** when an entry below carries a *durable* lesson (a coding standard, an architectural invariant, a hard-won gotcha), promote it to the relevant doc above; the entry stays here as the dated record of how it arose.

---

- **The rig ran, the rip was 14/14 — and our own parity tool said 13/14 (2026-08-04).** The maintainer ripped the EAC baseline disc on the `b4` + `c5fb909` pair and sent the log, addendum, cue, rendered EAC log, JSON and three `log.txt` files. Running our tooling over them rather than reading them found **four things, three of them ours.**

  **`scripts/eac_parity.py` reported 13/14 NOT parity for a rip that is 14/14.** It read track 5's CRC as `6902BCF0` — the pass our own auto-fix **discarded** after re-ripping — where the file on disk is `E0036697`, which is EAC's own value. **A false negative on the one number that answers "is Platterpus bit-perfect?", produced by Platterpus's own checker.** Reporting it unexamined would have told the fork we had regressed.

  **The sweep that exists to prevent exactly that had two holes, and the second is the instructive one.** `tests/test_rip_addendum.py` (a) globbed `src/platterpus/` only, so every tool in `scripts/` sat outside the rule — §5.o again — and (b) triggered on `parse_cyanrip_log`/`parse_rip_log`, so a module that opened a log and pulled per-track CRCs out of it by any **other** route was not *exempt*, it was **unseen**. Widening both immediately found `render_eac_log.py` and `rip_report.py` doing the same, so the **archival** EAC-compatible log and the regenerated report were exposed too. **A green check while three tools broke the rule it enforces.** The lesson is the trigger, not the file: a sweep that names *some* ways of reading a log will keep missing the next one.

  **And routing them through the sanctioned reader silently deleted their error path** — `read_any_log` never raises, so the `except OSError` that printed `cannot read <path>` and exited 2 became unreachable. Caught by an existing test within one run; the check is now explicit and before the read.

  **`--consumer` has never been sent, by any Platterpus, ever.** The log says `Consumer: not identified (no --consumer given)`. `_build_rip_argv` gates it on `consumer_tag_for_build(ripper_build_tag)`, that parameter defaults to `""`, and **nothing ever passed it** — so the capability, its allowlist and its sanity check were all built and tested around a value nobody supplied. The parameter's comment said defaulting to empty *"is what makes the safe behaviour the default rather than something a caller must remember"*, and no caller remembered. Every existing test called the argv builder **directly and passed a tag**, measuring the gate and never the wiring: `RipHandle.cancel` for the third time.

  **I had told the maintainer twice that `b4` fixed it.** I ran `accepts_consumer_flag('platterpus-fork-gc5fb909') → True` and concluded the flag reached the ripper — one half of the path measured, the other assumed. The rig log was the only artifact that could tell the difference. *Running half a path and inferring the rest is the same error as reading a claim and believing it.*

  **A25 closes as PASSED, and its premise had expired.** It held that our 89× pre-gap bug was hardware-unprovable because cyanrip reported `none` for all fourteen tracks. The fork's sub-channel pre-gap read changed that: ten tracks now carry a non-zero `Pregap LSN`, and track 2's `14327` against a true length of `160` is exactly the case. **A dated exemption outlived the condition that justified it** — and the screening command I had written into the session sheet inherited the same expiry, hitting on almost any disc where the discriminating string is `Pregap source: TOC`. Across 40+ retained source lines, **zero** say TOC, so the fork's C1 fix is recorded hardware-unprovable on this collection rather than untested.

  **Lap 23 is `docs/handshake/verified/round-07-lap-23.md`, and writing it found a hole in the shared spec.** With `HANDSHAKE-TESTED` declared for the first time and no substantive objection left, the file should have said GO — and our own conformance test refused it: *"declares GO but peer verdict is 'HOLD', not GO (§5)"*. The check is right, and **that makes a first GO unexpressible: both sides need a closable GO, a GO is closable only once the peer has GO'd, so neither can go first and an agreed round has no way to record it.** Round 6 closed before the wire header existed, so round 7 is the first to reach a close attempt under v2. Deliberately **not** fixed unilaterally — the spec is shared, and silencing a check that found a real problem is the most literal possible violation of *do not weaken a check to make your file pass*. Proposed for round 8 instead; the lap ships a **procedural HOLD** that says so.

- **`v0.6.4b4` cut, and the reason was a route rather than parity (2026-08-04).** The maintainer asked whether a Platterpus beta made sense now that the fork had rolled one. It did, but not for symmetry — and the argument only became visible by *running* it rather than reasoning: the wizard and `--install-ripper` read `WIZARD_TARGET`, **a constant that ships inside a release**, so on the published `b3` there was no in-app route to the fork's new test pin `c5fb909` at all, and a hand-built `c5fb909` would have had `--consumer` **withheld** (`accepts_consumer_flag` → `False`, a silent `Consumer: not identified`) with log verification `not_determined` — on the exact build the joint hardware session exists to exercise. That is the fork's own rule *"a moving pin needs a route to it that does not ship inside a release"* biting from the side neither of us had checked: **the route exists, but its target ships inside the release too.**

  **The same question, asked about their build, was answered by running the classifiers rather than reading them** — `identify_from_banner` → `kind='fork'`, `accepts_consumer_flag` → `True`, `accepts_verify_log` → `True`, `approve_ripper` → `unapproved` *with the test pin named in the detail*. All five true as of `b4` and none of them true of `b3`. Worth keeping as the shape of the answer: *"is X visible to us"* is a question with an executable answer, and the executable answer is the only one worth sending to the other project.

  **And a defect in the one string whose whole job is to be quotable.** `version_pair_line()` — the maintainer's *"include what versions you both are"*, rendered into the Copy-diagnostics bundle — printed **`cyanrip cyanrip 0.9.4-rc1 (…)`**. Two renderers both prefixed the tool name to a banner that already begins with it. **Both of its tests were green and could not have failed**: each asserts *containment*, and `"cyanrip cyanrip 0.9.4…"` contains `"cyanrip 0.9.4…"`. A containment assertion is structurally blind to a duplicated prefix — the *can this be satisfied by the wrong thing* question, arriving in the most-pasted string in the product. The replacement **counts**, over both renderers and all three banner shapes.

  **Two process lessons, both mine:** (1) the CI watcher I armed embedded Python in a bash heredoc, the escaped quotes survived into an f-string Python 3.11 rejects, and the monitor reported a `SyntaxError` **instead of** check results — a watch that says nothing about the thing it watches. Smoke-test the watcher before arming it. (2) Its replacement returned `TOTAL 0`, which reads exactly like "CI never triggered"; it was unauthenticated `api.github.com` returning an empty list for the repo. The real reason there were no checks is documented in `ci.yml`'s own comment: **a GitHub App token's pushes and PRs do not fire `push`/`pull_request` workflows**, and `workflow_dispatch` is the escape hatch. Two different causes with one symptom, and only the second was real.

  **`-s ours` needs its own proof.** Main's b3 squash was not an ancestor, so the merge conflicted; the fix is this repo's established *record-the-squash* merge, but `-s ours` **silently discards the other side**, so "it merged cleanly" is indistinguishable from "I dropped the b3 fix." Verified first: every line present on main and absent here is one the branch deliberately supersedes (version stamps, the test-pin constants, a renamed handshake file, a local sort replaced by the shared `sort_key`), and `step_engine.py` — the b3 fix's own file — is byte-identical. Then asserted the merge changed no tree.

- **Running the diff they asked for found we had been violating our own shared spec since the day it was written (2026-08-04).** Their lap 20 §I1 asked us to *diff* their three ordering rules against ours *"rather than agreeing in principle."* Two divergences — and the finding that makes the lap worth recording is **where the rules already lived**: `docs/handshake-protocol.md` §3 has said *"absent means lap 1"* and *"by declared number, never by filename or mtime"* since commit `fec0ca3`, the commit that created the file. Both of our implementations violated both, for the file's entire life. **A spec statement with no §8 conformance row is a statement that gets read.**

  **(1) The round half of `sort_key` read the filename** while the lap half already read the header — one sort key, two notions of where the fact lives. **(2) A lap-less file fell back to its name** rather than to lap 1. Neither reordered any file either project has ever had, which is why only a comparison could find them.

  **Their rule 1 needs a qualification before it goes in the spec, and our own record is the evidence:** 27 of the 41 committed correspondence files declare no `HANDSHAKE-ROUND` at all, because the v2 wire header begins at round 7 lap 2. *"Never the filename"* has to mean *never in preference to the header*, or the rule is unimplementable against the very record it governs — and both sides must already be falling back, since neither reports every pre-v2 file as round 0.

  **Removing the fallback created a fail-open state, and that state got the fix's own test.** A file carrying a v2 header but omitting its required `HANDSHAKE-LAP` sorts *oldest* under §3, so a later `GO` reads as the round's newest word while its `HOLD` sorts underneath. The answer is not to keep the fallback (§3 forbids it) but §2 rule 4 — *an absent required field fails closed* — applied at the **gate**, which had been reading these files without ever asking whether they were coherent. `ordering_blockers()` refuses that plus a declared-round/filed-round mismatch (§8 row 10), a state reachable *only because* the round half became header-first.

  **Then the same rename bit a fourth place, and this one was load-bearing.** `tests/test_argv_surface_agreement.py` — the check that exists because the `-V` blocker sat undiffed in a committed file for a round — grouped inbound files with the *grandfathered* round regex, which does not match `round-07-lap-NN.md`. **Every one of round 7's laps was invisible to it**, and it had been diffing our argv against **round 6's** flag table since 2026-08-04. Measured: the label read `round-6.md + round-6b.md + round-6c.md` while the fork was on lap 21.

  **The guard written to catch that had inherited the same blind spot** — it computed "the newest round on disk" with the same regex, so it read 6 — **and its final assertion could not fail**: `used_round <= newest_on_disk`, where the used round is *drawn from* the on-disk set. A check satisfiable by anything, guarding the check that would have caught a release blocker. Both replaced; the regression test now asserts the **production** grouping rather than a copy of it, which is what made the first version pass with the bug restored.

  **And a second half nobody can fix from here: none of round 7's twenty-one laps embeds a P1 flag table.** Every one points at `PROVIDER-CONTRACT.md @ <commit>` in the fork's repository, which is not present in ours — so the newest flag table we hold predates this round, while their lap 21 reports the count moving 40 → 41. Recorded as a ratchet (`_MAX_TABLE_LAG`) so the gap can shrink but never silently grow, and asked for in lap 22 §C3b.

  **Read off the artifacts, not remembered: one version string names four commits and three source anchors.** `cyanrip 0.9.4-rc1+platterpus.5-beta.1` is declared by laps 8–20 across `9003e6f`, `ceca8bc`, `f00cb2b` and `486dce3`, while `HANDSHAKE-SOURCE-ANCHOR` — the field whose whole job is to say *"`src/` moved"* — moved twice underneath it, and one of the changes it spans is a **log value we parse** (track 1's `Pregap length:`). We were unaffected because our classifiers key on the build tag, never the version; a human reading a report's `ripper_version` could not tell 150 from 300. Two of their headers also name a build **their own delivered artifact contradicts** (lap 12 says `g9003e6f`, its reference says `gceca8bc`; lap 14 says `gf00cb2b`, its reference says `g486dce3` — `f00cb2b` being the *committing* commit, which by their own lap-16 rule cannot be the producing build). The artifact's banner was the reliable witness in both cases.

  **The lap also found our generator emitting a file our checker refuses.** `--emit` omitted `HANDSHAKE-PROTOCOL`, the first entry in `REQUIRED_WIRE_FIELDS`; `check_outbound` sweeps sections rather than the header, so *"our skeleton satisfies our own spec"* was green throughout. `handshake_filename()` exists because a hand-typed **name** is a third description of a fact — and the **header** that instruction points at was hand-typed three definitions from the tuple defining it.

  **Lap 22 is `docs/handshake/verified/round-07-lap-22.md`**; the test pin moved to `c5fb909` (`beta.2`) with `9003e6f` retired-not-deleted, since it is what the rig actually ran. Round 7 remains **OPEN**, both HOLD, and `v0.6.4` stays planned.

- **Describing a loader's behaviour found two divergences a test on either side could not (2026-08-04).** The fork adopted the naming convention as-is and, in their lap 18 §B1, described how *their* loader orders files. Ours differed in two places, and **neither side had a test for either** — because one difference changed no observable behaviour and the other only bites in a case nobody had constructed.

  **(1) A no-lap file was lap 0 for us, lap 1 for them.** Identical ordering on both trees today, so nothing anywhere failed. Theirs is right: a round's pre-lap-header file *is* that round's first lap, and lap 0 invents one that never existed. Adopted, and our round-7 no-lap files renamed to `round-07-lap-01.md` to match theirs.

  That rename **collided with our own converse rule** (*"a canonical name on a file declaring no lap is a false label"*) — their file would have failed our checker. But under the lap-1 rule their name states a *derivable* fact, which is exactly what the rule exists to require. So the rule got refined rather than either side backing down: the exemption is bounded to lap **1** specifically and to being the round's **only** no-lap file, which is why round 6's three amendment files keep legacy names on both sides.

  **(2) An ambiguous `HANDSHAKE-LAP` fell back to the FILENAME for us — a real hole.** A file named `lap-09` declaring both 9 and 20 sorted at 9, a later valid file was read as newest, and **the ambiguity was never examined by the gate at all.** The protocol's own *present-but-ambiguous is worse than absent* principle, broken in the direction that hides it, inside the code that decides whether a round is closed. Their rule — ambiguous wins the sort — makes it the file the verdict is read from, at which point the header check refuses it by name.

  **The generalisable bit, and it is now a working practice rather than a coincidence:** three times this round a defect on one side sent the other to check the same shape and find something. The mechanism is not code review — neither project can see the other's tree. It is that **a described *behaviour* is checkable against your own behaviour in a way a described *intention* never is.** So when either side reports *how* something works, the other compares rather than acknowledges.

  **Their §B1 also named a test flaw better than I have**: their first ambiguous test asserted only that the gate refused, which passed with the rule reverted, *because an unknown lap is separately caught as ambiguous* — **"a second guard was answering for the first."** That generalises past this case: any test asserting an outcome two guards can produce measures the disjunction, not the guard.

  **And two of my own tests broke this lap, both the same way — pinned to tree state rather than to a property.** One floor required a legacy-named file in round 7; their rename removed it, so the floor **failed on a tree that was more correct**. Another located a committed artifact as `INBOUND_DIR / "round-7.md"`, pinning the filename rather than the artifact. Fourth time this round the *test* rather than the code needed fixing, and the pattern in all four is identical: **the assertion was easier to write than the property was to state.**

  **Their §C found where lap 14's wrong advice actually lived**, which is worse than what I reported: not prose in a lap file but *"a false claim inside the generated document"* — `PROVIDER-CONTRACT.md` P1 said **"prefer `--version`, it has never changed and never will."** Deleted, replaced by a `STATED, NOT DERIVED` section with the commits, per-row derived-or-stated marking, and a check whose two citations were each falsified in turn.

  **Lap 19 is `docs/handshake/verified/round-07-lap-19.md`.** It proposes one addition to round 8's already-agreed spec bump: the shared spec should state the **ordering rules**, not only the naming — no-lap is lap 1, ambiguous sorts last, ordering is `(round, lap)` and never the string. *A naming convention without its ordering rules is a format both sides can honour while reading it differently*, which is the state we were in for one lap without knowing. Round 7 remains **OPEN**.


- **A file naming convention, and the release gate it caught answering the wrong question (2026-08-04).** Maintainer directive: *"agree on a naming convention for the handshake files and both use it"*, then *"make sure you give cyanrip instructions… so they implement their side of it."*

  **The old scheme had already destroyed a file.** `round-N` plus the next free letter, and the letter encoded nothing: `inbound/round-7f.md` was **lap 12** while `verified/round-7f.md` was **lap 10**; two files were both lap 7 by coincidence; and filing their lap 12 **overwrote lap 4**, which had to be restored from git. That happened earlier in this same session. The convention is `round-NN-lap-LL.md`, which makes the filename a *second description* of what the wire header already declares — safe only because a test now asserts the two agree, plus the converse (a canonical name on a file declaring no lap is a false label, worse than a legacy name because it looks checkable). Pre-lap-header files are grandfathered by a **derived** rule, not a list: a file with no lap has nothing to name itself with.

  **Then the migration exposed something far worse than the naming.** Immediately after the rename, `--status` reported round 7's `they-verified=**GO**` while their lap 16 declares HOLD — **the release gate reading a close out of an open round.** The cause is exact: `_round_files` sorted by stem, and lexically `"round-07-lap-16" < "round-7"` (`'0' < '7'` at the seventh character), so the fork's **lap 1** file sorted last and was read as the newest.

  **Three independent copies of that ordering existed and the rename broke all three at once** — the script's, the argv-surface test's, and the one checking the wizard installs the nominated test pin, which began reporting the hardware pin as unnamed by the newest round. Fixed with one public `sort_key` on the *declared* round and lap rather than the string. **A sort key looks too small to share right up until it decides a release gate**, and "three copies of an ordering" is the same drift shape as everything else this round, just in a place nobody thinks of as a description of a fact.

  The suite caught it inside the same commit, and only because `--status` and two tests read the same tree — a convention change with no behavioural surface would have landed silently. Regression-tested with floors that require the mixed-scheme condition the bug needed (a legacy AND a canonical name in one round, and a lap ≥ 10, since below ten the two sorts agree), plus an assertion that the naive key *still* misorders the pair so the fix is not decoration. And the naming check was proven to bite by planting a `round-07-lap-99.md` declaring lap 15: three tests failed.

  **Their lap 16 settled J3 by building 0.9.3 and running it.** Our table's first row was right — `--version` does not exist on 0.9.3 — so **do not reorder**, and their lap-14 advice is withdrawn. The generalisable part is theirs: *"the two flags are exactly complementary across the stock line… any probe over stock builds needs at least two attempts by construction — no ordering makes it one."* That retires the optimisation rather than answering it. And their §I names why the wrong advice happened: *"prose about behaviour is worth less than we have been treating it as"* — with the sharper version being that their `cli` scenario pins all three spellings **for the fork**, a real test whose scope is narrower than the claim it was cited for.

  **They also killed an option we had offered as the cleaner fix.** Regenerating a golden reference inside the change's own commit would not collapse the two commit hashes: the artifact would be built from a tree whose HEAD is the previous commit, so the banner would name the parent again. *"It moves the mismatch rather than removing it"* — a description of a build cannot live inside that build, which is the same shape as their `Handshake-Lap` argument.

  **Lap 17 is `docs/handshake/verified/round-07-lap-17.md`**, with §C written as implementable instructions rather than a description: format, each rule with its reason, artifacts, what we ask of them, and the shared-spec question. The convention's natural home is `docs/handshake-protocol.md`, which **neither project owns** — so it is deliberately *not* edited, and proposed as a joint round-8 section alongside the machine-readable state. One spec bump, two agreements.


- **They confirmed the pregap finding from their source, and their answer disproved a claim in ours (2026-08-04).** Their lap 14: track 1's `Pregap length: 300 frames` was a real bug — the per-track block added the 2-second lead-in **unconditionally**, so a TOC that already signalled the gap got the same 150 sectors counted twice — and `150` is authoritative. Re-parsed the new reference: `{300,300,150,150}` → `{150,150,150,150}`, with track 2 unchanged at 75 throughout.

  **The part I could not have reached alone** is their exclusion of the alternative: *"track 1's pregap on a real disc **is** the lead-in, and an HTOA is audio recorded inside it. The two readings name the same sectors. They are never additive."* I was right to report the disagreement and right not to call it a bug; the resolution needed their source. And *"one block knew; the other did not"* — their `Gaps:` block had always drawn the distinction the per-track block missed — is the cleanest statement of why a **four-source cross-check** found it and a same-side test could not.

  **Kept both references rather than replacing one.** The superseded artifact is the only thing that proves the defect shipped, same reasoning as the unregenerated H2 `.platterpus.json`. And I measured the blast radius of their fix against our own parsed fields instead of accepting their §E delta — every disc and per-track field compared, only the two declared ones differ. *"We believe your delta"* and *"we checked your delta"* are different claims.

  **Their D2 disproved a range comment in our own source, and it was an inference I had labelled an inference and left in anyway.** `VERIFY_LOG_FLAG` said *"the checksum footer and this flag arrived together."* The footer's commit (`757108c`) **predates** the flag's (`443f749`), so **builds exist that write the footer and cannot verify it** — one lap after I wrote to them that we deliberately do not infer flag support from the footer. Their phrasing for the general shape is worth keeping: **"a flag added *inside a version number that never moved*."** Three faces of one trap now — a flag removed (`-V`), a flag not yet added, a flag added under a static version — and all three defeat a version-string check, which is why the support lookup keys on the build tag.

  **The stall count, and the gap it closed.** Lap 13 parsed their `Read stalls:` line and surfaced it **nowhere** — capture-without-surfacing again, in the same lap that fixed an instance of it. Their D1 published four exact shapes (derived from the code, `strcmp`-pinned, **not observed from any build**), so the count is now structured *as a layer on top of* the verbatim text: unrecognised wording yields `null` beside intact text, never `0`. A positive count raises an `issues[]` entry — a read that took 187 seconds matters even when every checksum is right. Their singular `1 read exceeded` is asserted on its own; a guessed `reads` would have missed it, which is the concrete payoff of asking instead of inventing.

  **Answered their J3 "not yet", which took more care than agreeing would have.** They asked us to send `--version` before `-V`. The fact half is theirs and correct: current stock rejects `-V`. The inference half — *"a rejection is the 'not installed' false negative your own detector exists to prevent"* — is real in general and **already mitigated here**: our probe loop tries every flag and reports absence only when all fail. So it is a cost question, and our table says stock 0.9.3 takes `-V` and not `--version`, and 0.9.3 is what is deployed. **But that row is our claim from reading upstream's source, not a measurement**, so lap 15 asks them to settle it — and says plainly that if 0.9.3 does accept `--version` the argument collapses and we reorder. Their own B2 gave the phrasing: reasoning can be sound while the conclusion does not follow.

  **One finding of ours, and it is rule 12's third instance.** The golden reference's banner has never once named the commit their lap file names it by — lap 12 said `70dcf19`, banner `ceca8bc`; lap 14 says `f00cb2b`, banner `486dce3`. Benign mechanism (fix commit builds it, later commit checks it in) but their §A claims the stronger thing outright. The **behavioural fingerprint** they proposed in round 6 is what settles it and it exonerates the binary: the new reference reports 150 frames, so whatever built it had the fix. We are not in doubt about the artifact, only the label.

  **Two of our own checks earned their keep, both by failing.** The parser's completeness sweep rejected the two new fragment patterns — and it carried a **test-side allowlist** naming one pattern as "the one exception", which is precisely the shape that hid 16 of their fatal strings behind their generator's prefix filter. The enumeration moved into the module. And a lap-13 test was **pinning a literal where a property belonged**: it asserted their note said `"lap 10"`, and their new reference says `lap 12` — a failure for a reason that is *correct behaviour*. Replaced with the actual contract, which is their own `Handshake-Lap` argument in assertion form: the compiled-in lap must be strictly behind the lap of the file that ships it. **A test that fails when the dependency does the right thing teaches people to edit the test.**

  **Lap 15 is `docs/handshake/verified/round-07-lap-15.md`** — HOLD. Round 7 remains **OPEN**; schema v19.


- **The measurement I offered found two things the design argument could not — one in our code, one in theirs (2026-08-04).** Their lap 12 called in the offer I made in lap 11: *run the real parser over the golden reference and report per-line.* I had answered their lap-9 J2 from the *design* (label-driven rule table, never positional, property test for never-raises) and stated the caveat honestly — a claim about the design is not a measurement. They sent the artifact and said *"run it and tell us."*

  **The design claim held. The measurement found two things anyway.**

  **`Read stalls:` was not parsed at all.** A line they added at their own initiative *for us* — and in lap 11 I had answered their question about whether we wanted it per-track versus disc-level, **about a line the parser was silently dropping.** My answer was right on its merits and given without the standing to give it. That is *"am I answering from the artifact, or from my memory of the artifact?"* applied to our own source, and it is the second time in two laps: I also described their pre-logfile behaviour as stdout-only when lap 9 had already changed it to a buffered-and-replayed block — which was *in the reference file I was holding.* The generalisation is sharper than either instance: **a design argument is evidence about the design, and a contract answer needs evidence about the code.**

  **And a finding about their artifact.** Track 1's pre-gap length disagrees with itself: `Pregap length: 300 frames` + `duration: 00:04.00` agree with each other; `Start LSN 150 − Pregap LSN 0 = 150` + their own `Gaps:` block's `150 frame pregap in track 1` agree with each other. Two internally-consistent pairs, exactly 2× apart. **Track 2 is the control** — all four sources agree at 75 — which is what makes it a finding rather than a suspicion about our arithmetic, and the audio geometry is not in doubt (150–374, 225 frames, matching its own `Frames:` row), so the question is confined to the gap. I did **not** call it a bug: a cue declaring `PREGAP 00:02:00` on top of a 150-frame lead-in makes 300 correct and the `Gaps:` line partial, and that is unfalsifiable from outside. Asked which is authoritative, and asserted the disagreement in a test that fails if either line changes — the disagreement is the record, and a silent change would erase the question.

  **Three traps we had already been caught by, exercised on real output for the first time.** `Accurip: disabled` with computed v1/v2/450 CRCs present → 0 verified, `neutral` verdict (F2); `Accurip 450: 00000000` → not a match (F3); and **`Pregap LSN: 0` → classified `known`.** That last one is the first artifact in which the zero case has ever appeared, and `0` is falsy — a truthiness check anywhere on that path turns a *reported* position into "not reported". It was right. It is now pinned.

  **Their J4 was a real finding about code I had written the day before.** Our `--verify-log` classifier told a rejected *flag* from a rejected *log* by matching cyanrip's error text. Their reply: that string is **genopt's, not theirs, and one upstream sync from changing** — key on the exit code plus the flag's presence in their published table instead. They were right, and it is a lesson I have written *to them* twice: **a matcher built on a dependency's prose is a hand-maintained list of shapes.** `failed` now requires positive evidence the build accepts the flag; tri-state `True`/`None`, never `False`, because no document says any cyanrip *lacks* it. The cost is named rather than hidden: `failed` is currently unreachable for stock cyanrip, which is the fail-safe direction, and closing it needs one sentence from them (asked as D2).

  **A floor caught my own first attempt at the derivation.** I looked for pins named *in the same file as* the flag table and found **zero** — their tables are in rounds 4–6b and their pin declarations start at 6c. `assert checked >= 2` reported it instead of passing while comparing nothing. Re-derived as *continuity*: every published table lists the flag and none has withdrawn it, which is their own range-of-builds lesson rather than one citation.

  **Worth recording about them, because it is the protocol working.** Their J5 fix was a hit: our lap-11 press on the `messages` cap (*"a tool's fatal message is the last thing it prints"*) found a head-only cap, and their fix chose **two fields over a synthetic elision entry** — *"a line the program never printed, inside the record of what the program printed."* That is a fair criticism of our own `captured_stdout`, which does insert a marker; defensible in a text blob, not in a JSON array of emitted strings. Their revert-proof — one failing check of six, and it is the last-line one — is a *measurement* of how invisible the bug was rather than a claim about it.

  **Lap 13 is `docs/handshake/verified/round-07-lap-13.md`** — HOLD, with `Handshake-Lap` accepted (their argument: *a file can never name a build that contains itself*, and the reference proves it — its note says lap 10 and it arrived with lap 12). Round 7 remains **OPEN**; report schema v17 → **v19**.


- **The fork read our two artifacts and found six things; five were real, and the one that mattered most we had already answered ourselves (2026-08-04).** Their round-7 lap 10 arrived with `H1`–`H6` and `J1`–`J6`. I verified every finding against the committed rig artifact before acting on any of it — CLAUDE.md's *"did a correction get less scrutiny than a claim?"* — and two turned out to be **worse** than diagnosed.

  **H1 — the addendum broke `cyanrip --verify-log`, and our own integrity check said the log was fine.** We appended the auto-fix supersede block to cyanrip's own `.log`, which ends with a `Log FUN512:` self-checksum that `--verify-log` rejects trailing content for. Verified by locating the addendum at line 1144, immediately after `Log FUN512:` at 1142. The record now goes to a sidecar and their log is byte-exact.

  Two things about H1 are the actual lesson. **The question had already been asked and answered — in round 5, by them, with a test pinning it** (`sc_verify_log()` writes a log with trailing content and fails if it verifies). They quoted their own test back at us. *A contract answer that lives only in a document is one nobody re-reads* — which is the sentence we wrote to **them** about their flag table two laps earlier, now pointing the other way. And **the fix had a trap in it**: the addendum existed because a *re-parse from disk* got the CRCs of bytes we had deleted (the GUI never sees it — it patches from live worker state), so moving the record out of their log without teaching the reader about it would have "fixed" H1 by making the supersede *invisible*. One wrong artifact traded for another. `read_log_with_addendum` is now the only sanctioned read-back and a source sweep enforces it, with a synthetic offender exercising the sweep's failure path.

  **H2 — a wrong verdict, not a terse one, and my own test from that morning hid it.** They reported `ripper_handshake_approval_detail` saying *"no build tag"* about a banner that plainly has one. Right finding, and they said so, wrong diagnosis. Running the real parser showed the mechanism is sharper: our parser **splits** the banner (`log_creator` without the parenthetical, tag in `ripper_build`), and `approve_rip_log` read only the half with no `(`. So it was reading *the other half of its own parse*, and the **verdict** was wrong too — `not_determined` where the same log's full first line returns `unapproved` and names the test pin. The maintainer's own earlier instruction had predicted the right value (*"expect `unapproved` on every rip"*) and we shipped the other one. **The test I wrote the same day passed because its fixture set `log_creator` to a *whole* banner — a shape the parser never produces.** The stand-in was more capable than the product. Regression now reads the committed log through the real parser, and the archived `.platterpus.json` is kept **unregenerated** with an assertion pinning the discrepancy: it is the only hardware evidence the wrong verdict reached a real report.

  **J3 was their best ask, and the reason is a general one.** *"Make `log_integrity` actually run `cyanrip --verify-log`."* Our check verified *the file we wrote* against *a checksum we computed* — **a closed loop agrees with itself no matter what we did to somebody else's file**, and it reported "the EAC-style log matches its own SHA-256 footer" on the very rip that shipped a broken cyanrip log. `adapters/ripper_log_verify.py` now runs their verifier over their artifact on every rip, on the worker's thread (the audit registry runs inside `write_report`, which runs in a Qt slot — a subprocess there is the freeze rule, three times in blood). The old check is **renamed `our_log_integrity`**, question *"does the log WE wrote match the checksum WE published?"*, and kept as a separate row so a pass on the easy one can never imply the hard one. A rejected *flag* classifies `not_determined`, never `failed`: the `-V` lesson pointing the other way, because a build that does not know the flag cannot be reporting a bad log.

  **H4 — our EAC log said 15 tracks on a 14-track disc.** `unverified = total - verified` already contained every offset-variant track, and the third line counted those same tracks again. Each line defensible on its own wording, the aggregate arithmetically false, inside a SHA-256-attested archival document. Fixed as arithmetic only — **wording untouched**, because they asked that neither side reword unilaterally and our log is what they diff. And the fix **created** a failure mode worth more than itself: with the counts disjoint, "could not be verified" is zero on a disc where everything matched only the +450 pressing, so a clean-sweep headline keyed on it would print *"All tracks accurately ripped"* over a disc where nothing matched exactly. It keys on `total - verified`, with its own test. *"What new state does this fix create?"* earning its place again.

  **The one I found by re-reading our own lap 10.** We told them *"when the two witnesses disagree, the disagreement is the finding"* about our banner verdict versus their compiled-in `Handshake:` note — and **nothing in the code compared them.** Parsed at v17, stored, claim published, never used. That is capture-without-surfacing one layer up, and it is the *identical* defect they found in `ripper_handshake_approval` (written correctly, consumed nowhere). Fixed. The false-positive half is what decides whether it survives a hardware session: the real artifact is unapproved with a note that agrees, and it must stay silent or every deliberate test-pin rip cries wolf.

  **§A, and the generalisable bit.** Lap 8 declared the tested pair as `0.6.4b1`; the rig ran b3. They caught it by hand, and their diagnosis is the finding: *"nothing in either artifact would have flagged the mismatch."* The evidence was in this repo (`generator.version`, the EAC header) and the declaration was three directories away, and no code related them. `tests/test_handshake_cites_the_pair_it_ran.py` now requires any handshake file citing `output_reference/<dir>/` to declare the app version **and** ripper build tag that directory's artifacts actually carry — both halves, because naming one from the artifact and the other from memory is round 4 repeating. It has a proof-of-failure case running lap 8's real `0.6.4b1` against the real b3 artifact.

  **Also closed a gap of the same shape in our published contract:** the generated consumer contract enumerated only the *rip* argv, so `--verify-log` and the version probes — our two most failure-prone invocations — were absent from the document titled *"flags we pass you."* The `-V` blocker one layer up. Both now derive from the same constants the code uses; §3 went 15 → 18 flags; and the argv-surface test splits **rip flags** (every one must appear in their published table) from the **version fallback tuple** (exempt for a stated reason, since no single entry works on every build), with a test keeping that exemption from widening to `--verify-log`.

  **Lap 11 is `docs/handshake/verified/round-07-lap-11.md`** — HOLD, all six findings answered, `J1`–`J6` answered including lap 9's five, and a concrete shape proposed for their machine-readable test-pin state (three closed-vocabulary fields *beside* the prose line, not replacing it — two witnesses is a feature here). Round 7 remains **OPEN**; `--release-gate` exits 1 and v0.6.4 stays planned, not cut.


- **The rig artifact found three defects a green suite could not, and closed EAC parity at 14/14 (2026-08-04).** The maintainer uploaded a real rip of the EAC baseline disc — b3 + `platterpus-fork-g9003e6f`, same drive, same offset — plus five rotated `log.txt` files. I ran our own parser, exporter and comparators over them instead of reading them.

  **The wins, measured against the committed EAC log rather than asserted:** every one of the **14 `Copy CRC`s is identical to EAC's**, which closes the `output_reference/README.md` parity goal; and **all ten of EAC's `Pre-gap length` rows match ours to the hundredth of a second, in order**. That second one closes the KDD-32 shortfall for the fork — and `_gap_handling`'s docstring was still describing it as open, so the doc was *understating* us. **Track 5 got there only via the auto-fix**: first pass `6902BCF0` (no AR v1/v2 match, only `+450`), re-rip `E0036697`, which is EAC's value. The re-rip feature earned its place on real hardware.

  **The three defects, and what they have in common.**

  1. **`Gap handling` was wrong on every disc with a pregap.** The fork prints `merging into track N`; we looked for `merged`. One word ending. The row read "(not reported by the ripper)" where EAC says "Appended to previous track". The fork **published** `merging into track %i` in round 5 — two rounds of it sitting in a committed file in this repo, exactly the `-V` blocker again. And the part I keep returning to: **`_gap_handling`'s own docstring already named this trap**, about the previous bug in the same function — *"its test could not catch it because the fixture handed the function a string cyanrip does not emit"* — and the replacement test did it again with a different invented string. **Two** tests pinned `merged`. Writing the lesson in the docstring did not stop it; the tests now read the fork's published block out of `docs/handshake/inbound/round-6.md`.
  2. **`artifacts.ripper_stdout` was empty on every completed rip.** Not a missing call — a *guard*: `ripper_stdout=... if self._rip_worker is not None else ""`. Someone knew the worker was cleared before the debounced re-writes and concluded "send nothing" rather than "keep the value". First write good, every later write blank, and FLAC verify + the self-check always trigger one. The block's `source` string still promised "complete even when the ripper was killed" — accurate about the mechanism, false about the file. This is the artifact the cyanrip project cannot make for itself and the one lap 10 tells them we capture.
  3. **`self_check` was an undeclared top-level key — and the sweep I wrote that morning could not see it**, because it inspected `build_report` while `self_check` is added by `write_report`. The real file had 32 keys where the builder makes 31. *"What does my stand-in do that the real thing does not?"* — it stops one function early. The sweep now writes a report and reads it back.

  **The shape all three share is the one this whole session has been about, arriving from a fourth direction: a fact obtained and then discarded, with an artifact that still looks complete.** And the meta-lesson is sharper than the fixes: **every one was invisible to a suite I had spent the day strengthening, and visible in ten minutes of reading a real file.** Two of them were pinned *by tests* — the fixture agreed with the bug. CLAUDE.md's *"my two witnesses are related"* and *"assert against the source artifact"* are the same rule seen from two angles, and this is the clearest case of it yet.

  **What the logs themselves gave up.** 35,559 lines, **two** warnings — and one of them was the gap-mode failure, logged by the very function that then reported "(not reported)". Our own diagnostics found the bug; nobody had read the log. The other was a real drive stall. Also: 35,522 DEBUG vs 30 INFO, which is G0 in the flesh — with Debug logging off that file would have been 30 lines.

  **Landed alongside:** the fork's `Handshake:` and `Consumer:` lines are parsed (schema v17). `ripper_handshake_note` is the binary's **own compiled-in** statement of the round it was built from — `round 7 lap 7 OPEN, verdict HOLD -- NOT a released build` — which is provenance derivable from the artifact's content and a second, independent witness beside our banner check. It was on TASKS as "currently unrecognised"; the artifact is what made it implementable, because a fixture would have been my guess at their wording. And the real-log sweeps are now **build-aware**: they were written when "the real logs" and "stock output" were the same set, which is precisely the state that let the fork's wording hide.

- **Full error reporting: four audits, and the finding was almost never "we did not know" (2026-08-04).** Maintainer directive: *"do a full check for error reporting to both Cyanrip and Platterpus… I want full error and reporting to the output log file (JSON) as possible for future debugging. Be thorough and verbose; make finding errors easy."* Four parallel read-only audits — subprocess capture, swallowed exceptions, the JSON report surface, user-facing surfacing — produced ~40 findings.

  **The shape they share is the whole lesson: they were not facts we failed to obtain, they were facts we obtained and threw away.** Which `CLAUDE.md` already calls the worse of the two, because the artifact still *looks* complete, so nobody investigates it. The three that read worst:

  - **A missing channel, not a missing call.** Three post-rip adapters declared their command seam as `Callable[[list[str]], int]`. An `int` — so the dependency's own words could not *structurally* reach the result, the report or the user, however careful any call site was. "⚠ FLAC verify FAILED for 3 file(s): a, b, c" named the files and could not name the reason. `adapters/tool_run.py` is that channel now, shared rather than per-adapter, because four facts drift to three the moment there are three copies of them.
  - **`metaflac` runs on every rip and logged nothing.** It is how edited tags reach the FLAC and how cover art is embedded. Argv, exit code and output all discarded at the point of failure; three of six call sites reduced the exception to one line and one dropped its text entirely.
  - **The report written for the most-broken rips carried the least.** Our rip-failure report exists for a rip that produced *no log at all* and embedded neither the ripper's `captured_stdout` — built with a head, a counted elision and a tail **specifically to survive a kill** — nor the session DEBUG buffer, while `log.txt` is INFO by default and every ripper line is written at DEBUG. The output existed in memory, in a variable the code already knew how to serialise, and reached **nothing**. The full-report path had passed both all along.

  **The fourth shape, and the one I would carry forward hardest: the two dialogs that tried hardest were the two that got it wrong.** ~20 failure dialogs said *"see the log"* and named no path; two named one, and both hardcoded a literal that is wrong under a relocated `XDG_DATA_HOME`. That is not twenty forgetful authors — *there was nothing to call*. Same shape as the fork's hand-maintained prefix allowlist in round 5: the effort was real and the **mechanism** was the problem. So `ui/failure_text.py` holds the sentence and `tests/test_failure_surfaces.py` sweeps for anyone writing their own; the sweep found six live sites the audits had not listed.

  **Two of my own new checks were wrong first, in the two ways this project keeps predicting.** The "see the log" sweep fired on a *comment documenting a fix* — satisfied by the wrong thing. The wiring sweep reported `ctdb_client.py` as unwired because its import shares a line with another name; it reads the AST now. **A matcher narrower than the language it inspects produces confident wrong answers, and a false failure trains people to ignore a check as surely as a false pass lets a bug through.** Graduated into `docs/error-reporting.md` §6.

  **A `NameError` on the setup engine's own failure path** was the sharpest single moment: the diagnostics call I had added referenced a module that was never imported, so a *failed* step raised from inside the code meant to explain the failure. Found by the suite, not by review. The reporting path is code too, and it fails like code.

  **What landed:** `diagnostics.py` (one collector, two sinks, four encoded rules) + a `diagnostics` block at schema v16; eight new `issues[]` checks, each a fact that could be true while the one list a triager opens first said "nothing to flag" — most sharply `recompress_failed`, since the step that rewrites archival masters was not even a *parameter* of the deriver, and the entire v15 handshake-approval block, which was read by **nothing**; `tool_run.py` with a third state (`started`) so a missing binary and a wedged file stop being the same value; full capture in `metaflac`, the cache probe, `eject`, `run_capture`, CTDB, MusicBrainz, cover art and the library move; `report_types.py` drift closed and its test turned from three hand-anchored blocks into a runtime sweep; **Help → Copy diagnostics…**, because the UI had no copy action at all and the one place a cyanrip fatal is displayed could not even be selected; and `docs/error-reporting.md` as the design of record.

  **Sent as round 7 lap 10** (`docs/handshake/verified/round-07-lap-10.md`, HOLD — the round stays open). It states our half so they can hold us to it, and asks three things back: confirm the same promise on their side; answer their own lap-7 §4 on the seven stdout-only refusal paths (**our view: document them as stdout-only rather than opening the logfile earlier — a logfile opened before the disc is validated trades an old ambiguity for a new one**); and state the **range** a contract claim covers, not the snapshot. I owed the third one too, so the generated consumer contract now opens with §0 naming exactly which app version and which approved ripper build its claims cover — at the accepted cost of a two-line diff on every version bump, which is not spurious because the document really does describe a new version.

- **v0.6.4b3: the `$HOME` that was never expanded — and the lesson is about *consistent* wrongness (2026-08-04).** b2 existed to make the fork-build failure visible. It did, and the answer was in the first log it produced. **Both of my earlier hypotheses were wrong** (a stale meson build directory; then nothing coherent at all), and the actual cause had been printing itself in plain text the whole time.

  `FORK_SOURCE_DIR` was the literal string `"$HOME/.cache/platterpus/cyanrip-fork"`, carrying a comment that said *"`$HOME` is expanded by the shell inside the container, not here."* **That comment was false.** The value arrived as a positional argument and the script did `src="$1"` — and **parameter expansion does not recurse**, so `$HOME` stayed five literal characters. Every path derived from it was *relative*, under a directory named `$HOME`. The user's log:

      Source dir: /home/rmccann/$HOME/.cache/platterpus/cyanrip-fork
      ninja: Entering directory `$HOME/.cache/platterpus/cyanrip-fork/build'

  **Why nobody saw it for months.** The build *worked*. Right commit, right version, 31 of 31 targets, installed, exported, all exit 0 — because the clone, the configure, the compile and the install all used the **same wrong string** and therefore agreed with one another. The only casualty was meson's `vcs_tag`, which cannot resolve a git revision from that path and falls back to upstream cyanrip's literal `release`. So the binary reported `platterpus-fork-grelease`: a build tag naming **no commit**, refused (correctly) by our verify.

  **The durable lesson: consistently wrong is the hardest kind of wrong to see.** Every internal cross-check agreed, because they were all reading the same broken value. This is the shared-ancestor failure (`docs/testing.md` §5.ac) in a new costume — my two witnesses were related, so their agreement carried no information. What broke the tie was an **external** check: meson's independent attempt to read git from that path, which failed and left a fingerprint. Graduated: *when every internal check agrees and the artifact is still wrong, look for the one consumer that does not share your assumption.*

  **The second lesson is one string.** The verify step said only *"does not identify as the pinned fork build (`platterpus-fork-g9003e6f`)"* — what it **expected**. It printed the observed banner one line earlier on stdout, and `_run_commands` keeps only the *last* line for the UI, so the single fact that mattered was discarded exactly when it mattered. Had the message quoted `grelease`, this ends in the first round: `grelease` does not look like a wrong commit, it looks like *no* commit, which points straight at `vcs_tag` rather than at the checkout. **A diagnostic that reports the expectation and not the observation is half a diagnostic.** Now both are named, and the fallback case gets its own sentence.

  **Also fixed:** a silently-succeeding command (`sudo install`) left *nothing* in the log between the argv line before it and the one after, so "did it run?" was unanswerable — every command now logs its exit. And the build step gained deliberately-excessive diagnostics: `$HOME`, cwd, resolved tree, requested pin, an absolute-path and `$`-bearing guard with named fatals, `HEAD` + short sha + `describe`, a dirty-tree warning, a probe of whether git works in the source root, and finally **the banner read off the binary it just built** — so a wrong tag is attributed to the build that made it. `assert_shell_safe_subpath()` puts the guard at the argv chokepoint, in code rather than in prose.

  **Process note worth keeping.** Three of my four confident mechanisms this session were wrong (build directory, then two dead ends), and one was right (the AppImage/PyPI resolution). What kept the wrong ones from shipping was labelling them *hypotheses* and naming the command that would falsify each. The revert-proof discipline also caught itself: a revert script asserted "expected 2 occurrences, found 3" and aborted **before writing**, so the green run that followed was the unreverted code — which is precisely the vacuous pass CLAUDE.md warns about, avoided only because the count was asserted.

- **v0.6.4b2: the first real use of the beta found three wrong messages in minutes (2026-08-04).** The maintainer installed `v0.6.4b1` on the rig and every finding came from *using* it, not from reading it. **All three were messages, not mechanics** — the code did the right thing and told the user something false about it.

  **The wizard said "✓ Setup complete — you can rip now." while listing "✗ Platterpus fork of cyanrip — installed cyanrip does not identify as the pinned fork build" two lines below.** One run, both sentences. The headline came from `is_ready()` alone, which is `cyanrip_exported() and flac_exported()` — pure **reachability**. Their *previous* fork build was still exported, so it answered `True`, correctly, to a question nobody asked; and because the verdict never read `results`, a FAILED step could not change it. **CLAUDE.md's "can this check be satisfied by the wrong thing?" answered by shipping.** The fix is tri-state, because two states are real: a user with a working older ripper *can* rip, so "setup did not complete" would be as wrong as "setup complete". **The gap that hid it is the durable lesson:** the pre-existing failure test used `ready=False`, where reachability and the results agree — *a test that only exercises the case where two signals agree cannot detect that the wrong one is being read.*

  **Every setup command's output was captured and thrown away.** `SubprocessRunner.run()` logged the argv and dropped stdout/stderr; `_run_commands` then reduced it to the **last line** for the UI. A failed `git`/`meson`/`ninja` inside the container therefore left one line of evidence in the entire system, and the log file we ask users to attach contained **none of it**. Found the only way it could be found: by trying to diagnose a real failure and having nothing to read. Captured-and-discarded is worse than never captured — the report still looks complete. Now exit code + argv + complete output, bounded head **and** tail with a counted elision marker.

  **The dependency dialog cannot answer the question the user asked it.** It says `cyanrip 0.9.4 (the Platterpus fork)` / `0 missing` — and said exactly that while the wrong fork build was installed, because `check_cyanrip` returns presence + version + location and never reads the build tag. Both fork builds share base version `0.9.4`. Every word true; the message wrong. The binary's own `--version` is the only thing that can distinguish them, which is what the maintainer used.

  **And a discipline note worth keeping.** For the fork-build failure itself I had a confident mechanism — meson's `vcs_tag` baked into a build directory reused via `--wipe` rather than removed — and said so *as a hypothesis*, with the command that would confirm it. It was **wrong**: removing the build dir changed nothing. Because it was labelled a hypothesis rather than a diagnosis, nothing shipped on it. That failure is still open, and b2 exists partly so the next attempt is diagnosable at all.

  **Two more found by reading, not running:** a modal message box parented to the main window appears *behind* a modal dialog that spawned it (the update prompt, which Plasma also refuses to raise because it is raised from a background worker rather than a click); and the Settings dialog is 35 form rows with no scroll area and no height cap, whose OK/Cancel row sat on the bottom edge of a 1080p screen — I added the 35th row. `centering.py` claims a too-large dialog keeps "the title bar and buttons reachable"; true for width, and for **height** it guarantees the buttons are exactly what is lost. Both filed, neither fixed yet, so the build under test does not shift mid-session.

- **Round 7 laps 7-9: the beta, and the release that had never been building from this tree (2026-08-04).** The maintainer's call was to ship a beta on both sides regardless of what the gate says, so the joint hardware session can actually run. Their laps 7 and 8 moved the test pin twice — `f750890` → `d9c7124` → `9003e6f` — each move retiring a build the previous lap had told us to install, and `f750890` for cause: its `-x` cache probe ran *before* the stall watchdog started, so a hang on the least-exercised read path in the program was silent, and H10 is precisely that run.

  **The release had failed twice, and the cause was that no AppImage has ever been built from the tree.** `requirements.txt` carried a bare `platterpus`; `PIP_FIND_LINKS` only *adds* a resolution source, so pip still picked the highest acceptable version — and **pip excludes pre-releases by default**. With a local `0.6.4b1` wheel and `0.6.3` on PyPI it chose `0.6.3`. Only the version sanity-check caught the mislabeled binary. **It was invisible for every previous release because the tree version and the newest PyPI version were always the same number**, so "built from the tree" and "built from PyPI" produced identical artifacts. That is the shared-ancestor failure (`docs/testing.md` §5.ac) arriving in the build system: two witnesses agreeing because they are related. A beta was the first version PyPI could not answer, and it took a *pre-release* to expose it.

  **`ForkTarget`, because the wizard was hard-wired to one pin.** Pointing the build target at a test pin was not a constant swap: the build step named `FORK_PIN` and the verify step named `FORK_EXPECTED_BUILD_TAG`, two constants that agreed only because one derived from the other. With a second installable build that becomes two independent edits, and getting one wrong installs one binary while checking for another. One object now carries the commit and the banner a correct build must print, and both steps read the same field off it. `WIZARD_TARGET` stays a *separate knob* from `FORK_PIN`: the deviation policy forbids moving the production pin while a round is open, and conflating "what we install to test" with "what a closed round approved" is how a test build becomes the production record by accident.

  **The beta update channel, and three defects the writing of it found.** The maintainer asked for auto-update to a beta *with a warning and an option*. What existed was worse than no option: the check took `releases[0]` unconditionally, so anyone who wanted an update was handed a tester build with nothing saying so. Building the channel surfaced (1) `0.6.4b1` and `0.6.4` comparing **equal**, because the version parser is the dependency-probe one where a trailing `b1` is correctly noise — a **one-way door**: a tester on the beta would never have been offered the final release, or the next beta; (2) selection by *publication time*, so a backported patch would have been offered as an upgrade, i.e. a downgrade; and (3) — the instructive one — **the channel cannot gate on GitHub's `prerelease` flag**, because this project marks every `v0.*` tag as a pre-release, so across the entire 0.x line that flag is a constant and gating on it left the stable channel with nothing to offer *ever*. I wrote the flag-based version first. It was caught by a test whose fixture is not contrived: it is simply what our releases actually look like. **A fixture drawn from the real artifact caught what a reasoned-about fixture would have missed** — the same argument as reading the committed file instead of remembering it.

  **A module written to satisfy a maintainer directive had no test of its own.** `handshake_approval.py` is the rip-time verification of *"verify at the time of rip as well so we can confirm"*, and the only thing touching it asserted the report *carried* its keys — not that the verdict in them was right. It would have passed a release gate while answering every question wrong. It now has a direct suite, including that `not_determined` never satisfies `is_approved` and that a `-dirty` banner cannot be an approval.

  **And the message, not just the verdict.** Against the test pin the honest answer is `unapproved`, which during the session it was nominated for reads as a broken install. The verdict is unchanged — softening it would discard the check — but it now says *why*: the current test pin names itself as nominated, a **retired** pin says it is retired and names the current one, and the pin under review says the round is open. Same lesson as the dependency dialog that read `cyanrip 0.9.3 / 0 missing`: every word accurate, the message wrong.

  **Lap 9 is information only**, per the maintainer — the Platterpus beta described and nothing else, mirroring the fork's own beta file rather than continuing the verification narrative. Round 7 stays OPEN, verdict HOLD; `2f950c8` does not move.

- **Round 7 lap 5: their conformance table found a defect ours could not (2026-08-04).** Their lap 4 arrived with `PROTOCOL.md` — the wire format as a **standalone shared document** — six answers from our outbound file, and pin `5bc654d` (r4). Four SHAs in one open round now; the gate is what has kept us on r2 through all of them.

  **The single most valuable thing in the exchange was running their §8 conformance table.** Fourteen rows a conforming gate must refuse, one it must allow. Ours failed **row 12: an empty record allowed a release.** `round_status()` returned a bare `"no handshake rounds recorded"` line; the gate decided by looking for lines ending in `OPEN`; that line does not; therefore nothing was open and it printed *"release allowed"*. **A gate satisfied by finding nothing — in the gate whose entire job is not being satisfied by nothing.** We would not have found it by *reading* the table. That is the argument for a shared table of cases rather than two prose descriptions of the same principles.

  **Their §2 rule 2 found a second defect, and our own test asserted the wrong behaviour.** A declaration is a statement a file *makes*, not one it *quotes* — and round files legitimately illustrate the header format in fenced blocks. Their gate read the example block in **our** lap-3 file and compiled an illustrated `HANDSHAKE-PEER-VERSION` into their binary as a fact about us. Ours had the identical hole and did not fire only by luck: our file illustrates `HANDSHAKE-VERDICT: HOLD` in a fence *and* declares `HOLD` for real, so the duplicate collapsed to the right answer for no good reason. Measured before the fix, our parser was also adopting `PROVIDER-CONTRACT` from inside that fence — a field we are not entitled to declare. And the part that is ours alone: the suite contained `assert wire_verdict("```\\nHANDSHAKE-VERDICT: GO\\n```\\n") == "GO"` with a confident comment about not parsing markdown. Confident, reasoned, wrong. **Three bait shapes now — indented, prose, fenced — and each project had found two of three.**

  **Protocol v2, and the correction we made on ourselves.** Lap 3 wrote our own §8 titled *"one language, both repos"* — and in doing so created a **second copy of the spec**, which is the two-vocabularies problem in miniature. Their standalone file is better because there is only one of it. Adopted verbatim as `docs/handshake-protocol.md` with a banner saying neither project owns it; our §8 now routes to it. Implemented: `HANDSHAKE-PROTOCOL` required with a higher version *refused* rather than guessed; their close set (both verdicts, both versions, both pins, `HANDSHAKE-TESTED`); a field declared twice → ambiguous, not last-wins.

  **H18 — their Q8 was a data-loss finding aimed at us, and the answer is no.** `cyanrip_log_init()` opens the logfile `"wb+"`, truncating, and the path does not vary with `-Z`/`-l` — so a second invocation with the same output settings overwrites the first pass's log. Ours cannot collide: `_auto_fix_tracks` runs pass 2 in a fresh `tempfile.mkdtemp(prefix="platterpus-refix-")`. That was guaranteed by a docstring and one incidental T14 assertion; it now has its own test. **We asked them not to change the truncation behaviour** — their recommendation (*"give pass 2 its own `-D`"*) is what we already do one level up, and a no-truncate mode would be a behaviour change for other consumers in exchange for nothing we need.

  **H19 — right finding, and the state was better than they assumed and worse than it should be.** `Done; …` is stdout progress; `Secure re-read:` is the contract line, backed by a three-state enum. We already parse both and the contract line already wins — but only because the in-block form is read *after* the track opener, i.e. **emergent from line ordering rather than asserted**. Three tests now pin it, one where the two lines deliberately disagree. `Done;` stays as a documented fallback because stock upstream emits no contract line, and stock is the ripper a user has before the wizard runs.

  **Two of their build failures went straight into our list.** Their `-dirty` work produced a test on a tree dirty *because of the edit under test* (cannot distinguish working from broken), and a `git diff` run from a gitignored build directory (reports clean forever). Both are *"a check that passes for the wrong reason"*, neither was on our list of ways a revert-proof can silently no-op, and both are now — credited.

  **Also found, unrelated and ours:** `test_regex_bounded_time`'s detector-proof was flaky — 10.9x growth measured on a *linear* pattern against an 8.0x ceiling, one run in three. Wall-clock noise only ever makes a measurement longer, so a single noisy sample in the denominator inflates the ratio without bound. Min-of-3 now; 5/5 stable at 29s for the file. A gate that fires at random is a gate people switch off.

  - **Lessons (durable):** (1) ***A conformance table is run, not read.*** Fourteen concrete cases found a defect that four prose statements of the same principle had not, in a gate we had just written and revert-proven. (2) ***Publishing a spec inside your own document creates a second spec.*** If two projects share a format, share the *file*; a faithful restatement is still a copy that can drift. (3) ***A declaration is what a file states, not what it quotes*** — and the corollary is that a format's own documentation is the most likely place to trip its parser. (4) ***A timing threshold on a single sample is not a measurement.*** Take the minimum; noise is one-directional.
- **Round 7 lap 3: two gates, two vocabularies, one protocol — and the fix the maintainer named (2026-08-04).** Their lap 2 arrived. It moved the pin again (`345241b`, third SHA for one unreleased version, both new commits prompted by our own file), confirmed our `Duration:` sign finding and **generalised it further than we had** — at `-s -667` it is *track 1* that inverts, the mechanism being `setup_track_lsn()` shifting both ends while a boundary clamp removes the shift at one end only — and then measured what the naive repair actually does: `+1 +1 −1` minus a frame is `0 0 −2`. **The shortcut does not merely miss the boundary track; it doubles its error.** Stronger than our version, and it is the one recorded.

  **The most urgent thing in their file was that we had never sent them `outbound/round-7.md`.** They asked three times. Q8 — which we cited three times as blocking our own addendum fix — was in it. Our protocol says two files per round; we sent one and then spent a lap answering questions they were reading in paraphrase. Delivered with lap 3.

  **They declined a remedy we proposed, and they were right to.** We had asked for one line: *"provider contract: unchanged from round 6b."* Measured, the contract had moved — flags 38 → 39 with `-x`, derived rows 422 → 431 — so we asked them to file "unchanged" for something that changed. Our *finding* (a checker blind to an absent §I) was right; our fix was a request to write a false sentence. Their replacement is better and is now symmetric: a generated contract at the pin, staleness a test failure, and a resolvable `PROVIDER-CONTRACT: <path> @ <commit>` pointer instead of a claim in round prose.

  **Then the maintainer set the standing rule, and it exposed that both projects had built the same gate in two languages.** *"Both of you should not make a new release until you are both happy with the handshake files… This needs to be an affirmative handshake and include what versions you both are and what to use, and verify at the time of rip as well so we can confirm."* Auditing against that found three real gaps:
  - **Our gate read half the contract.** `--status` read *our* verdict and not theirs, so their `HANDSHAKE-VERDICT: HOLD` could not block our release. **Third time in this protocol's life** for the one-half-of-a-two-half-contract error (round 4's flag table, round 5's fatal inventory, now the verdict). A round now closes only when both declare `GO`; four combinations tested, one closes.
  - **Two vocabularies.** They built `tools/release-gate.py` reading column-0 headers; we built `--release-gate` reading bolded prose. Same four properties, same day, same protocol, **neither able to read the other's files** — which is exactly what rule 12's *"this rule lives in both repos"* exists to prevent, arriving in the tooling instead of the prose. Theirs wins on the merits (machine-readable, survives rewording, a round number *in* the file cannot silently disagree with the filename) and is adopted. Specified as protocol §8 and **reproduced verbatim in the round file**, because they do not have this repo and "see §8" is a pointer into a tree they cannot read.
  - **Nothing checked the ripper at the drive.** A release gate runs once, on a machine that never rips a disc. `ripper_identity` answered *"fork, stock, or undetermined"* — a different question from *"the build both projects verified"*, and answering the second with the first is how an unapproved build would have read as fine. `handshake_approval.py` + report schema v15 now put the answer in every rip's own archived artifact, tri-state, with `not_determined` explicitly not a pass.

  **Two findings against our own tooling, both from writing their rules down.** (1) Requiring all ten sections of a *mid-round lap* was over-strict — their lap-2 reply legitimately has no golden log and no commit table, and our checker reported 20 problems against it. `HANDSHAKE-LAP` makes that decidable rather than a judgement. Over-strictness is the failure whose usual fix is switching the checker off. (2) `report_types.py` calls itself *"single source of truth for the structure `rip_report` writes"* and was missing **17 keys** across two blocks — eight shipped in v13/v14, and one (`ripper_argv_first_pass`) added by me earlier in this same cycle. A `TypedDict` that under-describes a dict literal is not a type error, because the emit site is not annotated as the `TypedDict`.

  **And a correction to our record that changes a rule.** The `HH:MM:SS.mmm` → `MM:SS.FF` duration-shape change is **upstream's** (PR #130), inherited by the fork; we had it filed as theirs. It is the *second* measured instance of the rule the `-V` removal taught — an upstream change cannot be escaped by rolling back to upstream — so `CLAUDE.md` now carries both, because one example reads as a special case and two read as the pattern.

  **Their caveat on T14 applies to our fixture too, and we annotated rather than hid it.** Their `reference` scenario reaches `Done; (no matches found…)` by exhausting the repeat limit on clean audio — *the right string for the wrong reason* — and they asked both sides to say so rather than let the fixture imply non-convergence was exercised. Ours has the identical defect. The docstring now states it outright and names what would fix it: hardware.

  - **Lessons (durable):** (1) ***Two implementations of one protocol will diverge in vocabulary before they diverge in behaviour*** — and the divergence is invisible while each side only ever reads its own files. Publish the wire format, put it *in* the exchanged artifact, and have both parsers implement the same tie-breaking rules (unknown verdict, two verdicts, empty record). Graduated to CLAUDE.md rule 12 and protocol §8. (2) ***A gate that runs in CI does not run where the failure happens.*** The release gate and the rip are different machines; a provenance check belongs at both. (3) ***Over-strictness is a real failure mode with a specific fix people reach for: switching the checker off.*** A checker needs to know the difference between a full round file and a reply. (4) ***A remedy can be wrong while the finding is right*** — and the other side declining it is the system working, not friction.
- **Round 7, lap 1: they measured the thing I had explained (2026-08-03).** The fork's round-7 return arrived proposing pin `d5d12ec` (`0.9.4-rc1+platterpus.3`) and explicitly asking us to *hold* rather than close. We replied with `docs/handshake/verified/round-7.md` — a mid-round lap, not a GO. **Round 7 stays OPEN, the pin stays `2f950c8`, and neither project releases** until both verifications are in.

  **Their §7 is the best finding in the round and it is against me.** The maintainer had asked why the v0.6.3 rip *"seemed much faster"*, and I answered by explaining the dynamic secure-rerip: pass 1 with no `-Z`, 12 of 14 tracks verified at confidence 200 first time, only the two misses paying the `-Z 2` cost, 1.195× extraction. All of that is true, and none of it answers the question. They **timed both sessions**: 81m11s and 81m13s. Nothing got faster. I checked their claim against our own artifacts and confirmed it — the *previous* session's pass 1 also ran without `-Z` (`-s 667 -o flac -r 5 -N -c 1/1`), so the mechanism I described had been in force both times. I had explained a mechanism and let the explanation stand as the cause of a delta I never measured. Owned plainly in §6 of the reply rather than softened.

  **Their §5 reproduced on our rig, with a sign flip they had not reported.** At `-s 667` the `Duration:` line is off by one CD frame — and the direction is not uniform: **+1 on tracks 1–13, −1 on track 14**. So the obvious repair, "add the missing frame back", silently corrupts the boundary track. The fix has to recompute duration from `Samples:`, which is exact. H2 answered *no* — we never parse `Duration:`, so no shipped artifact carries the error.

  **H1/H5 answered no, with a corroboration they could use.** Zero heartbeat lines across all five app-log rotations. But our own stall detector fired at `01:25:02,298` and `01:45:15,681` — *their exact timestamps* — plus a recovery at `01:38:55,984` that their reconstruction did not have. Their thread-based fix arrived at the same architecture we run for the same reason, from the same failure, independently.

  **Gate 1 verified exactly** (13 sub-channel entries, 1 lead-in, zeros on tracks 3/6/11/12, 9 `Gaps:` rows) — and flagged as an existence proof, not a range: one disc, all-zero pre-gaps on non-first tracks, so the non-zero case is still untested (H9, hardware-gated).

  **Their §6a proposed two mechanisms and it is neither.** The `Secure re-read` discrepancy is our own *measured* pass-2 result; the gap is that the archived addendum records only the swap that succeeded, not the attempt that did not converge. Blocked on Q8 (whether their `-Z N -l <tracks>` run writes a citable logfile of its own).

  **Their §6b is half wrong and the half that is right got fixed.** They read our `Defeat audio cache: Yes` as an echo of cyanrip's `Cache model:`. It is not — it is a measured `cd-paranoia -A` probe (KDD-29) and the parser is asserted never to fill that field from the ripper's log. But a bare `Yes` in an EAC-shaped log is indistinguishable from an asserted one, so the row now names its own method inline. Refuted as to mechanism, accepted as to presentation.

  **Two of our own defects, both from the round machinery rather than the product.** (1) I set `FORK_PIN = "d5d12ec"` and the release-gate test caught it — the deviation policy forbids switching pins while a round is open, and there was nowhere to record a *proposal* except the pin itself. Now there is: `NEXT_PIN_UNDER_REVIEW` / `NEXT_VERSION_UNDER_REVIEW`. (2) Their round-7 file has **no §I provider contract at all** — genuinely absent, not relettered — and `test_argv_surface_agreement.py` read "the newest round" unconditionally, found zero published flags, and failed *as though our argv had drifted*. It now walks back to the newest round carrying ≥ 30 flags and names which round it measured against.

  **And the round's own gate said the round was closed.** `handshake.py --status` reported `round-7: … verified=yes -> CLOSED` and `--release-gate` printed *"every round is closed — release allowed"* — because it counted the verification **file existing**, and round 7's file declares `**HOLD`.
