A release would have been permitted with the round open, which is the single thing the deviation policy names outright. This is §5.ad's defect one function over: that round's fix went into `--check`, and `--status` never got it. The gate now reads the verdict; `HOLD` and a verdict-less file both fail closed; rounds 1–3 predate the convention and are grandfathered *by number* in a set a test pins to exactly `{1, 2, 3}`, because "add the round to the exemption list" would otherwise be a one-line close. The marker is line-anchored because round 7's own second paragraph says *"not a closing GO"*. Revert-proven: reverting one expression makes the gate allow the release and fails four tests.

  **Then a documentation-currency sweep found three maps that had quietly expired, all three declared canonical by prose and enforced by nothing.** (1) `docs/README.md` — `CLAUDE.md` calls it *"the canonical annotated index"* with a parenthetical promising the list *"can't drift from it again."* That parenthetical was the whole enforcement and it had already failed twice over: `cyanrip-consumer-contract.md` was in `CLAUDE.md` and absent from the index, and **`docs/handshake/` — 24 files of binding release correspondence — was reachable from neither.** (2) `PLANNING.md` §2 promises *"one paragraph per module"* and was missing **19 of 122**, including `hard_exit.py` and `ripper_identity.py` — modules `CLAUDE.md`'s own rules name by name. A contributor sent to the module map to learn what `ripper_identity` does would have concluded it does not exist. (3) `docs/handshake/README.md` named exactly *one* round, four rounds in, and taught the presence-only closing rule I had just fixed. All three fixed, and all three now **gated**, because a map is only ever wrong by omission and omissions are invisible in a diff. The gate for the docs index requires an *annotated row*, not a mention: a document mentioned once in passing prose has a resolving link and is still lost, which is why the existing link gate could not see any of this.

  **T14 landed, and its own tamper case found a new defect.** The multi-pass end-to-end test I offered the fork in §9: a real `RipWorker` over a two-call fake ripper, pass 1 → AccurateRip miss → `-Z 2 -l 3,5` → report written to disk, **re-read**, run through the real audit. Floors first — the second pass must actually have run and the two argvs must actually differ, or a fake that collapsed to one pass makes every downstream assertion pass while proving nothing. Writing the "does it still catch real tampering?" case then exposed that the argv-agreement check matched **single-letter flags only**: `--injected-by-a-wrapper` appearing in the ripper's `Invoked as:` line passed as agreement. The check exists to catch exactly that. Widened to short *and* long options (never *values* — `-s 667`'s `667` and argv[0]'s resolved path legitimately differ), revert-proven with the reverted file re-parsed first so a stale import could not fake the run.

  **Preparing for their next file, in the two places it actually helps.** (1) The three artifacts we owe them are all hardware-gated, and the maintainer is the hardware — so they are now **§F of the hardware checklist**, written as commands rather than as intentions: F3 (the forced-error corpus) is five one-line invocations, needs no disc for the first, writes nothing, and is near the top of the if-you-only-have-an-hour list because round 7 cannot close without it. Three lines in `TASKS.md` were never going to get run. (2) **Readiness tests for both shapes their reply can take** — an amendment to the open round, or a fresh round opened out of order — plus a test that reads their committed round-7 file and asserts `--check` still names its absent §I, so when they supply it the *test* confirms it rather than my reading the file and forming an opinion. The last two rounds each surprised the tooling once; this is the cheap way not to be surprised a third time.

  **And the read-after-reap invariant got a test it never had.** cyanrip's logfile was block-buffered, so a killed process lost up to a 4096-byte stdio block (round 1, reproduced against a real cancelled rip whose log ended mid-token at `REPLAYGAIN_TRACK_GA`). The fork's `setvbuf` fixed the source; our half is *ordering*, which was already correct and entirely untested. The fake now writes the log from inside `wait()`, so an inverted order finds nothing — verified by inverting it.

  - **Lessons (durable):** (0a) ***Verify a write with the string you inserted, not a paraphrase of it.*** Three times this session a post-write assertion failed while the write had succeeded, every time because my needle dropped a backtick the inserted text contained. Harmless in direction (a false alarm, not a false pass) and pure waste in practice — and it dulls the check that exists to catch the *real* silent no-op. (0) ***A test written to prove a fix works is also the best place to find what the fix missed.*** T14 was written to cover a known seam; it found an unknown hole in the check it was covering. The "does it still catch the real thing?" half of a regression test is where that happens — the half easy to skip because the bug was a false *positive*. (1) ***Explaining a mechanism is not measuring a change.*** "Did I reproduce the symptom, or only explain it?" has a second failure mode I walked into: reproducing the *mechanism* faithfully while never establishing the *delta*. "This seemed faster" is a claim about two runs; answering it requires both, and a plausible account of one run is not evidence about the pair. (2) ***A rounding defect's sign can flip on the boundary element*** — verify a correction at the edge, not in the middle, or the repair derived from the common case corrupts the exception. (3) ***A gate that fails by misattributing the fault is worse than one that degrades.*** A missing provider contract is the other side's problem; a test that renders it as "your argv changed" spends the reader's time on the wrong repo. (4) A proposed dependency pin needs its own field — the live pin is not a scratchpad, and a test enforcing the round gate will (correctly) fight you for using it as one.
- **v0.6.3 met a real disc, and the artifacts had two bugs in them (2026-08-03, post-release).** The maintainer ripped a 14-track pressed CD on the released v0.6.3 against fork pin `2f950c8`, asked why it *"seemed much faster"*, and handed over the cue, both logs, the JSON and five app-log rotations. The speed answer was in the argv: two invocations, the first with **no `-Z`**. Dynamic secure-rerip reads every track once at max paranoia, checks AccurateRip, and re-reads only the misses — 12 of 14 verified at confidence 200 first time, so only tracks 3 and 5 paid the `-Z 2` cost. 49.9 min of extraction for a 59:42 disc = **1.195×**; total 1h 21m including the auto-fix pass. Uniform `-Z 2` would have read all 14 tracks three times.

  **Two defects, both the same shape: a report describing a *multi-pass* rip with fields that assume one pass.** (1) The argv-agreement self-check compared the *last* invocation's argv against the `Invoked as:` line the *first* pass writes, so it reported *"the command line changed in transit … Something between us altered it"*, naming the auto-fix `-Z`/`-l` as injected. On a clean rip. That false alarm fires on precisely the rips a user examines closely, and it accuses their system of tampering. (2) `Done; (no matches found, but hit repeat limit of 5)` — track 3 giving up — was scraped into `outcome.failure_hint` on a rip whose status was `success` and exit code `0`, telling every consumer and `--audit-rips` that this was *why the rip failed*. Both fixed; the second is the surfacing rule working and the *framing* being wrong.

  **What I checked and did NOT change.** The whole-disc log says track 3 `Secure re-read: not attempted` while the EAC-style log says *"re-reads did NOT agree"* — which looks like a contradiction and is not: the first is true of pass 1, the second is a **measured** negative from pass 2, and the app log has it outright (*"track 3 still didn't read identically even after an automatic re-rip"*). Parser and renderer are both correct. The real gap is that the *archived* addendum explains only the track that was swapped **in**, so nothing reconciles the two documents for a reader — captured and not surfaced, our own rule, and now an open task blocked on a question to the fork.

  **Real-hardware confirmation of the round-6 finding, from the case a fixture cannot reach.** Pass 1 ran `-Z 0` and all four disc-level paranoia counters equal the per-track sums *exactly* — READ 22055, VERIFY 1600, FIXUP_ATOM 54, OVERLAP 468. So the invariant the fork claimed in round 5 is real and its condition is `-Z 0`, exactly as their source predicts. Neither side's fixture could have shown this: at `-Z` off the sum is arithmetically forced, and the `-Z 2` references break it by the read count. Also: `FIXUP_ATOM` is non-zero on exactly three tracks and two of them are the two that failed AccurateRip — first evidence the counter tracks something, and still not a threshold.

  **The doc consolidation, done under a gate built first.** 79 markdown files → 64: four EAC investigations into `eac-parity.md`, engine-strategy + soft-fork into `cyanrip-fork.md`, ask-list + PR-roadmap into `cyanrip-upstream.md`, nine `output_reference/` READMEs into one, and the dated audit into `archive/`. Every merge candidate had 5–19 inbound references, so the *prerequisite* was `tests/test_doc_links.py` — 200+ relative links across the tree and nothing had ever checked one resolved. Two things would have gone wrong quietly: heading demotion had to be **fence-aware** (`cyanrip-soft-fork.md` contains `# inside the ripping container` in a shell block), and my first numbering collided with the sources' own — they number from 0, so `§2.1` was ambiguous between wrapper and source. Redone as lettered Parts with all 21 inbound `§N` references rewritten.

  - **Lessons (durable):** (1) ***A cross-check that fires when the product's own self-heal runs is worse than no cross-check*** — it lands on the rips people trust least and blames the wrong party. Compare like with like or not at all. (2) ***A field's name is part of its contract:*** `failure_hint` on a successful rip is a category error even when the string in it is true and useful. (3) ***`git checkout` to undo a revert-experiment restores from the INDEX*** — which did not contain my unstaged fix, so the revert-proof silently reverted the change it was proving. Fifth measured way for an edit to vanish; assert the file changed by re-reading it, always. (4) Build the gate before the refactor it protects, not after.
- **Round 6, three pins in one day, and two witnesses that turned out to be related (v0.6.3 continued, 2026-08-03).** The fork's round-6 return arrived, then an *urgent* round-6b hours later withdrawing the pin round 6 had just asked for, then a short r2 pin file moving it again. Verified all three against their tree and their artifacts. Round 6 is now CLOSED both directions on pin `2f950c8`; v0.6.3 ships with it.

  **Their finding first, because it is the best thing in the round.** At any paranoia level above 0, ripping a **disc image** returned one correct sector followed by silence — 99.7% of samples zeroed — while reporting `Ripping errors: 0`. Inherited from upstream (`c431d58` set paranoia's cachemodel, which doubles as its `c_block` read-chunk size, to 1 sector for image drivers). **Real drives were never affected** and no rig rip is in question. What matters is *why nothing caught it for a whole session*: their audio-safety harness compared the fork against upstream across 55 checksum lines and 11 decoded-PCM hashes and found them **identical, truthfully** — because both builds carried the same inherited defect. Two implementations that share an ancestor share its bugs, so equality between them is not correctness. Their suite never ran the broken path either (its `rip()` helper pinned `-P 0`; the harness did not), and *silence compares equal to silence*. One `cmp` against the source `.bin` found in a second what a session of diffing could not. Graduated to `docs/testing.md` §5.ac and to CLAUDE.md's pre-flight questions — this is the third direction we have hit the "my two witnesses are related" shape from, after the fixture/matcher shared ancestor (§5.ab).

  **Our exposure was luck, and it is recorded as luck.** The affected round-5 reference never entered our fixtures because round 5's §4b had asked them to *keep* the previous reference rather than replace it — on coverage grounds. Right call, wrong reason. Now deliberate: the committed reference is asserted to carry `-P 0` in its own `Invoked as:` line, so a future fixture generated without it fails at the point of entry rather than parsing perfectly and meaning nothing.

  **Five findings are ours.** (1) **Two consecutive golden references carry a build tag that is not the build** — banners three commits behind the stated pin, both *provable*: round 6's contains a log line absent from its named commit's source; round 6b's logs a read-chunk size introduced two commits later. `vcs_tag` reports HEAD, not content, so a dirty tree or a stale configure silently names a different tree. That reopened `--dirty`, which both sides had filed as "agreed, not asking". **And the second reference's counters are how I proved their fix is in the binary at all**: paranoia's READ callback fires once per `c_block`, so per-track counts went 225/150/75 → 15/10/5 = `ceil(frames/16)` — the fix, visible in the artifact when the banner could not say so. (2) **Per-track paranoia counters are per-*pass*, not per-track.** Round 5 told us they sum to the disc totals and I verified it — on a `-Z`-off artifact, where the sum is arithmetically forced. Read from their source, the baseline is re-snapshotted inside the `repeat_ripping:` loop; both their own `-Z 2` references break the invariant by exactly the read count. (3) **Their §C7 was refuted by their own Appendix 1**: it declared `REPLAYGAIN_TRACK_PEAK > 1.0` unexercised while the attached artifact carried 1.005757 and 1.033086 and a `True peak level: 0.3 dBFS`. They reached the same conclusion independently in 6b. (4) **Their success message was swept into their failure inventory** — `Done; (%i out of %i matches…)` under `goto finalize_ripping`, whose sibling `Done; (no matches found, but hit repeat limit of %i)` *is* a real diagnostic under the same label. (5) **The r2 pin file's justification is wrong and its pin is right**: `2f950c8` changes only `Changelog.md` and `README.md`, and `git rev-parse 2f950c8:src` equals `25a2265:src`, so the last commit that changes the binary is `22de22f` — but the pin decides the *banner*, which is what identifies the release and what our verify step matches. Pinning it is an identification choice, not a compilation one.

  **And one finding is a defect in my own gate.** `handshake.py --check` reported **one** problem in their round-6 file. There were three, and a fourth section passed on a coincidence: §G ("Revert-proof") was satisfied by a section they had lettered `## G. Asks back` while "revert" appeared **zero** times; §B's provenance markers were absent; and §I ("Provider contract") matched the prose *"I wrote, of your continuation-line sweep:"* — the contract really was in the file, so the **verdict was right and the reason was not**, and it would have passed with the appendix deleted. A section key must now sit in a real heading position *and* its subject must appear in the document; the regression test reads the committed round-6 file rather than a synthetic one. (Their amendment then supplied the revert-proof unprompted, so the *pair* satisfies §G — which is also why `--check` now takes several files: requiring an amendment to restate all ten sections would make sending a correction within hours score worse than folding it into the next round.)

  **Shipped alongside:** `platterpus --install-ripper` — the wizard's steps from the terminal, driving the same step engine rather than a documented shell snippet. The pin moved three times in one day and the wizard that installs it lives *inside* a release, so a user on the previous build had no in-app route to a newer ripper at all. Also: G1 answered with the seven still-`wording` sites at the new anchor plus the single missing derivation rule that covers six of them (`return <err-var>` / `return NULL`, where their recognised set is all *literal* failure values), and the seventh separated out as the window cut's false negative.

  - **Lessons (durable, all graduated):** (1) ***Two implementations agreeing is not either one being correct*** — assert against the source artifact, and add a non-triviality floor, because empty equals empty (`docs/testing.md` §5.ac, CLAUDE.md pre-flight). (2) ***Can this check be satisfied by the WRONG thing?*** — the companion to the floor question, and the one never asked of the gate guarding the handshake. Where a check matches a label, require the subject too (§5.ad, CLAUDE.md). (3) ***Did I verify this where it could have failed?*** — an invariant confirmed under conditions that guarantee it has not been tested. (4) ***A build tag names a commit, not the content built*** — provenance must be derivable from the artifact; a behavioural fingerprint beats a banner (CLAUDE.md rule 12, KDD-34). (5) ***A moving dependency pin needs a route that does not ship inside a release*** (KDD-34). (6) A fixture that pads with filler teaches the product that filler counts.
- **A release blocker found by reading their contract, and a green test that was measuring the wrong thing (v0.6.3 continued, 2026-08-03).** The cyanrip fork's round-5 return arrived. Verifying it — nine independent checks, each followed by an agent whose job was to refute the first — produced two findings worse than anything in the file itself, and both were ours.

  **Every version probe we ship would have reported the ripper missing.** All four sent `cyanrip -V`. That is right for 0.9.3 and wrong for everything after: upstream commit `442de2a` replaced the option parser and deleted `case 'V':`, so `-V` became an unparseable argument exiting 1 — and a non-zero exit from a version probe is deliberately read here as *"this tool is not available."* The launch dependency check would have announced **cyanrip missing** immediately after the wizard successfully built and exported it, and the wizard's own post-install verification would have failed on a perfect build. Not a fork regression: it would have hit stock upstream 0.9.4 identically, which means *"roll back to stock"* was never a mitigation. Probing now tries `-V` then `--version` — the minimal set spanning 0.9.3, stock 0.9.4 and the fork — from one module that also generates the wizard's in-container shell snippet, so the shell and the Python cannot disagree. The fork independently restored `-V` as an alias at a new pin; we keep probing both, because their alias fixes their binaries and not the 0.9.3 installs users have today.

  **The evidence had been in this repo for a full round.** Round 4's P1 flag table lists `-v`/`--version` and has **no `-V` row**. I had verified their *log lines* against our parser and never their *flags* against our argv — one half of a two-half contract. `tests/test_argv_surface_agreement.py` now does the input half mechanically, and with the pre-fix flag set it **fails against round 4's own table**: it would have caught this a round earlier without reading a line of cyanrip source. That is their ask J10, and they were right to make it.

  **The second finding is the more interesting one.** Our strongest-looking test — *"every string the ripper can print is surfaced"* — was asserted against the fork's **own machine-generated** 88-string fatal inventory. It was green. It was measuring their *filter*: their generator passed candidates through a hand-maintained 21-word prefix allowlist, and their round-5 control-flow re-derivation took the inventory to **104**. Re-derived here at both pins: 104 both times, strict superset, nothing lost. Our pattern missed **all 13** matchable strings the allowlist had hidden — including `Offset is unset!` and `Device does not support changing speeds!`, ordinary hardware failures that reached the user as a bare "Rip failed." The fixture and the code under test shared an ancestor: two guesses at *"what does a diagnostic look like"* that guessed alike. The matcher is now compiled from the ripper's published `printf` formats, 103/104 covered, 0 false positives, with the one unpatternable bare `%s` named and asserted rather than skipped.

  **Their §H1 correction was right and I verified it rather than accepting it.** My round-5 §1 claimed their generator could not see continuation-line format strings, and that "exactly these two, and nothing else" were missing. Both strings are in round-4's P2 at lines 1084 and 1094 — generated by the same parse, so a parse that could not see them could not have listed them. Their `LOGCALL`/`STDERRCALL` are `re.S`. Applying their actual 21-word allowlist to their tree reproduces the 88 **exactly** (set equality, empty symmetric difference). My sweep found 6 continuation-line sites, 4 of which *were* in P5 — so the shape existed but was not causal, which is a scoping error, not a factual one. Withdrawn. One sentence of theirs is refuted in return: *"there was nothing of that shape to find"* is false, and the difference matters because it is the kind of claim a contract inherits.

  - **Lessons (durable):** (1) ***A fixture inherits the blind spot of whatever produced it*** — "machine-generated by the other side" is strong provenance and the wrong question; ask what *filter* ran between their behaviour and the file. Graduated to `docs/testing.md` §5.ab, with the corollary that **raising a floor is not fixing a vacuous test**: this one's floor was `>= 80` and 88 cleared it comfortably. The population was the problem, never the number. (2) ***If a contract has two halves, check both.*** Graduated to CLAUDE.md as a pre-flight question and to rule 12 as standing tooling. (3) ***State the range a contract claim covers, not the snapshot.*** Their `"-v is version; there is no -V"` was true when written and one commit from being the misleading kind of true — the same shape as our dialog reading `cyanrip 0.9.3` / `0 missing`. (4) ***An upstream change cannot be escaped by rolling back to upstream.*** (5) The revert-prove check now has **four** measured ways to silently no-op, three of them mine this session: a formatter-reflowed anchor, a patch script asserting after it edits so the write never runs, and `ruff --fix` deleting an import between two halves of a change — plus the fork's own, where suppressed build output let a **stale binary** pass the test. Prove the file changed, prove the build succeeded, prove the behaviour differs, *then* believe the run.
- **We were never on the fork, and the dialog that should have said so said nothing (v0.6.3, 2026-08-03).** The maintainer sent a screenshot of the dependency check — *"7 ok, 0 missing/needs-attention. Installed: cyanrip 0.9.3…"* — and asked *"I thought we were using my branch of cyanrip. Shouldn't there be a different version and a distinguisher?"* Both halves of that question were right, and the second is the better one.

  **They were on stock cyanrip, and had been for every rip.** The container switch was a manual step in a test plan, and a manual step is a step that does not happen. The fix is not a clearer note: **the setup wizard now builds and installs the pinned fork** — build deps read off the fork's own `src/meson.build` at the pin (as `pkgconfig()` provides, so Fedora's `ffmpeg-free-devel` and RPM Fusion's `ffmpeg-devel` both satisfy it), clone, **detach onto the pin**, compile, install to `/usr/local/bin/cyanrip`, re-export, then **verify the installed binary prints `platterpus-fork-g<pin>`**. It runs *after* the stock COPR install, so a failed build leaves a working ripper rather than none. A test reads the newest closed handshake round and asserts the pin the wizard builds is the pin the record approved.

  **The distinguisher was the real bug, and it was mine.** `ripper_identity` had been wired into the EAC-style log, the JSON report and `--doctor` — and not into the launch dialog, which is the one surface a user reads. It printed a bare version, and a version is *the one fact that cannot answer the question*: the fork keeps upstream's version string byte-for-byte on purpose (`meson.build` sets a separate `PROJECT_FORK_ID`). "Enforce a rule across the codebase, not at the place it was learned" — again.

  **Then the maintainer's own EAC-vs-Platterpus tag diff paid for two more findings.** They ran it twice; the **first run printed only a track-1 header and nothing else**, and an advisor read that emptiness as *"a 1:1 clone of EAC's tagging"*. The second run showed a large, real diff. A comparison whose failure output is indistinguishable from its success output is the same defect as a check that can be satisfied by finding nothing — and it was answering a question about *our* tags, so: (1) a multi-disc rip was writing **`DISCNUMBER=2/3`**, the ID3 convention, into a Vorbis comment, and losing the disc total — we folded it into cyanrip's `-a` string when cyanrip has a dedicated `-c disc/totaldiscs` that sets two separate integer keys; and (2) the value is now **range-checked at the argv chokepoint**, because cyanrip refuses the *entire rip* on a bad `-c`, exactly like the `-t 17=` that killed a real rip in two seconds.

  **The first real-hardware run of the embedded `self_check` audited itself and found four silent checks.** It reported 8 checks run, 0 skipped, 6 findings — `pregap` and `argv_agreement` said nothing, because a stock rip has neither the rows nor the `Invoked as:` line they read. Auditing for the shape found `medium` and `log_integrity` too, plus `disc_identity`, which spoke but graded a *success* as a note, so `worst` could never read `ok` for a flawless rip. Each now says why it has nothing, and `run_checks` carries a floor so a future silent check is impossible rather than discouraged.

  **Implementing `-c` meant reading the fork's source at the pin, which found two fatal strings its own generated inventory never emitted** — `discnumber %i is larger than totaldiscs %i` and `Cover art already specified for track idx %i!`. Not two typos: in both calls the format string sits on a **continuation line**, so a generator scanning for a literal on the same line as `cyanrip_log(` cannot see it. A sweep of their whole `src/` for that shape finds exactly those two, so the inventory is 88 → 90 and the fix to their generator is bounded. Our surfacing is 90/90. Round 5 reports the *class*, not the instances.

  - **Lessons (durable):** (1) ***I verified their description of their behaviour and called it verification of their behaviour.*** Round 4 said their 88-string inventory was "VERIFIED INDEPENDENTLY"; I had verified the 88 strings *they gave me*. Re-deriving from source found two more. Graduated to the shared rigour bar as its own line, distinct from the artifact-vs-memory rule. (2) ***A gate in the wrong place is not a gate.*** "No release while a handshake round is open" was enforced only by a unit test asserting every round was closed — which blocked ordinary commits the moment round 5 opened, and never ran on the release path at all. The gate now runs in `release.yml`; the test now asserts the record's *shape* (an open round may only be the newest — the round-3 bug). Its predecessor failed by pinning "round 4 is OPEN"; this one failed by forbidding progress. Same family, third variant. (3) ***The revert-prove check caught a vacuous run for the sixth time — and this time the test was innocent.*** My first revert of the dialog fix *passed*, because the `str.replace` I used to revert it silently no-op'd: the formatter had reflowed the line I was matching. **Verify the revert landed before believing what the run tells you.** Third silent `str.replace` no-op this project has hit.
- **A correction I accepted without checking, three capture holes, and a protocol that now runs (2026-08-02, continued session).** The cyanrip fork's round-3 handshake, then round 4; the maintainer's questions turned out to be the load-bearing part.

  **§H2 was wrong and I shipped it before opening the file.** The fork argued EAC derives `Pre-gap length` from `INDEX 00 → INDEX 01` alone, so its track-1 `Pregap length: 300` (lead-in 150 + declared TOC 150) was not EAC-comparable. I accepted it, citing *my own* earlier re-measurement — "EAC reports no pre-gap for track 1, 9 of 14" — and changed the parser. Then I opened `output_reference/EAC_flac/eac_baseline_police_classics.log`, which prints `Track 1 … Pre-gap length  0:00:02.00`. That is 150 frames, the bare lead-in, on a disc declaring no track-1 gap: EAC's row **is** lead-in plus declared gap, the fork's original number was right, and my "9 of 14" was a correct count of `INDEX 00` lines in the **cue** — where track 1 structurally cannot appear — quoted as evidence about the **log**. Reverted. The fork independently retracted the same finding in round 4 §H2, for the same reason, having also never opened the log.

  **"Is there any output or error the log file does not capture?"** Three holes, and all three were facts we *had and discarded*, which is worse than facts never obtained because the report looked complete either way: retained stdout was **head-only** past 20 000 lines (a ripper's fatal message is its *last* line, so the one line a runaway needed to keep was the one guaranteed to go); the **exit code** was computed and dropped, so `1`, `0`-plus-cancel and `-9` rendered identically; and the **argv** was never recorded at all, which is why the `-t 17=` failure had to be diagnosed from files the maintainer uploaded by hand.

  **The multi-disc bug was worse after the symptom was fixed.** Every path took MusicBrainz's `medium-list[0]` under a comment reading *"the first medium is the one we want in nearly all cases"*. The argv chokepoint added earlier stops the resulting `-t 17=` — and stopping it converts a loud two-second failure into a **complete, successful-looking album of the wrong disc's titles**. `medium_select.py` resolves by disc ID, then a *unique* track-count match, then sole-medium, and reports "not determined" rather than flipping a coin when two media are indistinguishable.

  **The handshake became executable.** It was prose, and a round had arrived missing a required section twice. `scripts/handshake.py` emits our outbound file, validates theirs (including the two failures worse than a missing section — present-but-empty, and a null case left silent), and reports each round OPEN or CLOSED. It found two gaps on first run, **both ours**: round 3 was never verified back, and the return spec had grown A–I → A–J unannounced.

  **Round 4 closed in both directions, and their generated provider contract earned its keep on the first read.** The fork replaced its hand-written §I with a generated one — 37 flags, 241 stable log lines, 12 explicitly-unstable, the 88-string fatal inventory — and it immediately surfaced a shape no artifact either of us holds: `Rip completed:  no (interrupted by user, 2 of 3 tracks)`. My pattern handled only the `yes` form, so a **cancelled** rip parsed the verdict and dropped the ripper's own counts — on the exact scenario this whole line of work exists for. Their golden reference is a *successful* rip and could never have shown it. Three more variants likewise: a second `unknown` pre-gap reason (`sub-channel CRC mismatches`), and the `lead-in` and `sub-channel` pre-gap sources. A standing test now asserts we parse **nothing** on their unstable list — zero overlap today, and that is where the next breakage would have lived. Shipped as **v0.6.1**.

  - **Lessons (durable):** (1) ***Answer from the artifact, not from your memory of the artifact*** — and name *which* file, since the pre-gap convention flipped twice in one day on a true measurement of the wrong document. Graduated to `CLAUDE.md` and `docs/testing.md` §5.u, whose test **derives** the convention from the committed log and cue every run rather than restating it. (2) ***A correction from another project is not pre-verified.*** §H2 arrived as "you got this wrong" and was applied faster than any finding made in-house, precisely because of that framing. (3) ***Capture without surfacing is the same bug from the user's side*** — 21 of cyanrip's 45 fatal strings were captured and never shown. Now 88/88 against the fork's own generated inventory, committed as a fixture. (4) ***Fixing a symptom can make the underlying defect more dangerous***, and the medium-selection bug is the clean example: the guard was still right to add, but it was never the fix. (5) The revert-prove check caught a vacuous suite for the **fourth** time — 25 medium-selection tests stayed green against the reverted client, because they exercised the pure selector and never the wiring.

- **The single uploaded file became sufficient — and two more "the log's own list" bugs fell out of a real cancelled rip (v0.6.0, 2026-08-01, continued session).** The maintainer ran a partial rip on the rig and sent four files. Two findings came straight out of reading them, and the third came out of *how many files it took*.

  **A SHA-256-attested document contradicted itself, sixty lines apart.** The EAC-layout log for a rip cancelled after two tracks of fourteen said `*** INCOMPLETE RIP (cancelled) — this log covers 2 of 14 disc tracks. ***` on line 10 and **`All tracks accurately ripped`** on line 68 — both inside the checksummed body. `_status_report` decided its clean-sweep sentence by comparing the AccurateRip total against `len(rip_log.tracks)`: the log's own track list, which a cancel shrinks, so both sides of the comparison shrank together. **The fourth appearance of that exact denominator** (after the trust banner, the JSON verdict and the in-progress report write), and the concept that fixes it — `expected_track_total`, the number the rip was *asked* for — already existed and simply was not threaded that far.

  **Three "open" buttons could silently do nothing.** `QDesktopServices.openUrl` returns False when nothing claims the URL, and three of the four call sites threw that bool away. The worst is the file viewer's own *Open externally…*: that dialog exists **because** a bare `.log` has no handler on a fresh KDE, which is precisely when `openUrl` refuses — so the escape hatch was dead on the systems it was written for. The window's Help → Open logs folder had always handled it, *inline*, which is exactly why the other three never did. Now one shared `ui/external_open.py`. I could reproduce the mechanism but **not** the maintainer's report itself (their file associations are unknown to me), so the fix ships with a new log line that will settle it next time rather than a claim that their symptom is gone.

  **"Just assume I can only upload the json file."** Every diagnosis this session began by asking for a second file. Schema **v12** answers that: `artifacts` embeds the verbatim text of cyanrip's `.log`, the EAC render and the `.cue` (text only, extension-allowlisted — an audio path is refused and the refusal recorded, critical rule #8), and `completeness` finally *records* the disc's track count instead of merely consuming it. Writing the EAC log now re-arms the debounced report write, without which the one uploaded file would say `eac_log.exists: false` about a log sitting beside it.

  - **Lessons (durable):** (1) ***The denominator bug is not a bug, it is a shape*** — "the log's own list used as the population for a completeness claim", now four instances. The general fix is not another guard but *recording the denominator as data*: `completeness.tracks_expected` exists so a fifth surface has a number to read instead of a `len()` to compute. Graduated into `docs/testing.md` §5. (2) ***A rule enforced at the place it was learned is enforced nowhere else.*** The `openUrl` fallback existed, correct and inline, in one of four call sites — the same §5.o shape as the QThread-teardown rule that was enforced for one class out of nine. When a fix is a *behaviour* rather than a value, move it to a shared callable in the same commit, or it will not travel. (3) ***An artifact that cannot be diagnosed alone will be diagnosed with whatever the user happens to attach.*** The report was 164 KB of excellent structured data and still lost to a 4 KB text file it did not contain.

- **The cyanrip fork reported back, and its output found four bugs in Platterpus — three of them live on stock cyanrip (v0.5.21, 2026-07-31, continued session).** A parallel session developing the maintainer's cyanrip fork sent a status report. Rather than reading it as a confirmation, I reconstructed a log from its sample block, ran it through the real parser and both renderers, and ran a 124-agent verification workflow over the result: 34 findings survived adversarial verification (5 refuted), deduping to 13 fixes. **All 13 landed, each revert-proven, with reference-parse invariance proven by dataclass snapshot diff.**

  **The worst one had nothing to do with the fork.** cyanrip's `Pregap LSN:` row prints the ABSOLUTE LSN where INDEX 00 begins; Platterpus stored it in a field named `pregap_sectors` and every consumer rendered it as a LENGTH. So the error scales with position on the disc. On the committed reference pressing, track 2's INDEX 00 sits at LSN 14327 against `Start LSN 14487` — a 160-sector, **2.13 s** gap, exactly what real EAC 1.8 reports — and the SHA-256-attested log would have said **3 m 11 s**. An **89x over-claim**, latent for three releases only because the reference disc's TOC declares no pre-gaps, so the row had never had an input to fire on. Now derived as `start_sector - pregap_start_lsn`, which is precisely how cyanrip computes the duration it prints itself.

  **Why three green tests sat over it is the transferable part:** all three pre-gap tests constructed `TrackResult(pregap_sectors=N)` with a value that was *already a length*. They verified the formatter — correctly; it is still 10/10 against the real EAC baseline — and skipped the parser seam the bug lived in. A test that starts downstream of the defect cannot see it.

  **Two more false claims, both live on stock.** `Accurip: disabled` rendered as "in DB, no match", asserting both that the disc is in AccurateRip and that our read disagreed with it — because we inferred "a comparison happened" from the presence of a local CRC, on the written belief that cyanrip only prints per-track `Accurip vN:` rows when the disc was found. **It prints them in every state**, so the predicate was unconditional and `AR_STATE_ABSENT` was structurally unreachable. And an all-zero `Accurip 450` CRC was a confidence-200 match, although cyanrip prints the caveat itself ("a checksum of 0 is meaningless").

  **Our own advice caused the fourth.** v0.5.19's fork spec asked for the `-Z` `Done;` verdict to be indented, and defined "indented ⇒ belongs to the open track". The premise was that the verdict was stdout-only and absent from cyanrip's log file — **false at 0.9.3 and at master**, as the fork proved by reading `cyanrip_log()`. The fork indented the string *in place*, still inside the pre-opener repeat loop, so indentation and position disagreed and every verdict shifted one track: a converged track inherited the next track's failure, producing a false "not reproducible" AND a false "Test and Copy CRC identical" in the same log. The discriminator is now position. The auto-fix **file swap** gates on that boolean, so it was one ordering away from copying a read that never reproduced over good audio; it now refuses unless the re-rip's log describes exactly the requested tracks.

  **Six more fork-fidelity fixes**, the sharpest being that the fork's per-track `Gaps:` enumeration was truncated to its first line AND flipped EAC's Gap handling row to the *stronger* claim — because the sense test looked for the literal word "none", absent from `0 frame pregap in track 1, unmerged`. That is the v0.5.18 category error reintroduced by a **ripper wording change** rather than a code change.

  **Three of my own mistakes, caught by probes before commit** — two were the guard causing the bug it prevents: a zero-CRC check that treated an *empty* CRC as all-zeros (would have discarded genuine whipper matches); a percentage-preferred flag not reset per track (one track's percentage would suppress the next track's reading); and `return [], []` in a function returning `None`.

  - **Lessons (durable):** (1) ***A test that starts downstream of the defect cannot see it.*** Three pre-gap tests fed the renderer a value that had already passed through the broken step. When a bug is in a *conversion*, the fixture must hold the conversion's INPUT — the same shape as the 2026-07-30 finding where a fixture held the renderer's expected output. Graduated into `docs/testing.md`'s Definition of Done as "drive the regression test from the external text, not from a dataclass". (2) ***A floor equal to the population it measures is not a floor*** — third and fourth instances this week (`examined >= 14` against a 14-item roster, a sweep timing 3 of 98 patterns, a state table hardcoding its four known states). Assert against coverage of the population, never a constant that moves with it. (3) ***A spec that asserts a mechanism must say what the assertion rests on.*** Our indentation ask was specific, actionable and wrong, so the fork implemented it faithfully and it broke something. Had it read "because we believe the line is stdout-only", the fork would have caught it before writing the commit. (4) ***"Correct by accident" is a defect even when today's output is right*** — and a comment claiming an invariant nothing maintains is worse than no comment, because it stops the next reader checking.

- **v0.5.19's own blind spot shipped a worse bug than the one it fixed (v0.5.20, 2026-07-31, continued session).** An adversarial verification of the release I had just published found a **rip-aborting** bug in the one module the release's new sweep could not see, plus a false "Bit-perfect" the trust banner could show. Both were the same *class* v0.5.19 had just closed, in places the fix never looked.

  **The rip-aborting one.** v0.5.19 guarded eight `int()` conversions across eight parsers, where a raise degrades a field to "unknown". It missed `workers/rip_worker.py`, where `_progress_for` parses cyanrip's **live stdout** from inside the read loop, wrapped in a `try` whose handler terminates the child and emits a rip error. One unparseable progress line would have killed the rip *and* the disc read, minutes in. Eight sites guarded, eight patterns bounded. The percent needed a *separate* guard for a reason worth remembering: **`float()` does not raise on a long digit run — it returns `inf`**, which passes every check and then raises `OverflowError` inside `int()` on the GUI thread, in a queued slot, i.e. a crash dialog over a progress bar.

  **Why it was invisible is the actual finding.** The sweep shipped with `assert examined >= 14` against a hand-maintained **14-module** roster. The floor equalled its own length, so it could not fail for a module nobody had listed — which is the one thing a floor exists to prevent. Adding `rip_worker.py` and decoupling the floor surfaced all eight sites at once. *The guard-scepticism lesson graduated last session was correct and I then shipped a fresh instance of it in the same release.*

  **Two more of my own tests were decoration.** `tests/test_regex_bounded_time.py` — the "durable output" of the performance pass — skipped any pattern whose single `.search` came in under the 200 µs noise floor. Measured by restoring the skip: it collected 98 patterns, **timed 3**, and reported a clean sweep. It now times a repeated batch so every pattern gets a real figure, with a floor that fails on a skip *and* a self-test requiring it to still separate a known-quadratic pattern from a linear one. And the CHANGELOG bullet claiming the conversion "now routes through one shared guard" was untrue as shipped — `cyanrip_log.py`, the module the hole was *found* in, kept its private copy.

  **The banner was one sentence assembled in two places.** `set_rip_log` wrote the AccurateRip verdict; `downgrade_verdict` wrote verdict-plus-reasons; whichever ran last won. `set_rip_log` carried a comment promising it re-applied recorded downgrades, and **nothing did** — correct only by accident, because it happens to run once per pane reset today. One renderer now reads both inputs every time.

  **Also shipped:** report schema **v10** — five per-track facts the parser had been reading and the JSON dropped. `appended_silence_frames` is the one that matters and it is *not* fork preparation: cyanrip 0.9.3 already prints it (last track, overread off; it is on track 14 of both committed reference rips) and it states that the track's final frames are **fabricated silence rather than disc audio**. The machine record of an archival rip was quietly less complete than the human-readable log beside it. Its test asserts against the real reference rip, not a fixture — a fixture would have been written from the same wrong belief that produced the omission.

  **Delivered:** a cyanrip-fork confirmation file (exact line shapes, a runnable self-check whose expected output is pinned, and the three constraints under which Platterpus *refuses* a peak rather than printing a wrong one), and a rewritten `docs/hardware-test-checklist.md` with six passed sections retired and five new tests — A20 (a deliberately contaminated album folder) being the highest-value one on the sheet. `docs/cyanrip-upstream.md` had described four sections' shipped Platterpus readers as *future work*, which is exactly backwards for a document written to be handed to a fork author.

  - **Lessons (durable):** (1) ***A floor equal to the thing it measures is not a floor.*** `examined >= 14` against a 14-item roster, and `len(patterns) >= 40` while timing 3 of them — both passed while looking at almost nothing. Assert against *coverage of the population*, not against a constant that moves with it. (2) ***A parser's blast radius decides how hard to guard it, and "never raises" is not one tier.*** The same defect degrades a field in eight parsers and **kills a rip in progress** in the ninth; the roster that scoped the rule had no notion of that. (3) ***`float()` is not the safe sibling of `int()`*** — it returns `inf` instead of raising, so the failure surfaces later, elsewhere, and on another thread. (4) ***"Correct by accident" is a defect even when the output is right today.*** Both the banner and the report gap were one reordering away from being wrong, and in the banner's case a comment asserted the invariant was maintained. A comment claiming a behaviour the code does not have is worse than no comment: it stops the next reader from checking.

- **Six parallel refactor passes, and the two most valuable findings were about guards rather than code (v0.5.19, 2026-07-31, continued session).** Asked for "at least 2 more complete code refactors" for compliance, bug control and performance — *"in that order"* — plus "making sure intent is correct". A blind full-codebase refactor would have raised risk against the stated priority, so the passes were evidence-led: measure where bugs actually cluster, then act. Six agents ran; every fix was verified by reverting it.

  **The refactor that was correctly *not* done.** A bug-risk map (git-history fix counts, AST complexity, coverage, duplication) concluded the dominant shape is **one fact rendered by four surfaces that each re-derive it** — ~18 of the last 62 shipped `Fixed` bullets, the clear plurality. Mechanical duplication was measured and is *not* the problem (12 AST duplicate groups, nearly all benign siblings). The recommendation was therefore ~40 lines of test, not a split: `tests/test_surface_consistency.py` already existed for exactly this, and **its docstring named four surfaces while its imports had three**. Both missing surfaces held a live bug. The classifier now lives once in `verdict.py`, so the test's job becomes *exhaustiveness* rather than agreement-by-coincidence.

  **The intent pass found "never raises" was false in six parsers.** CPython's `int()` refuses a string over 4300 digits, so `int(match.group(...))` on an unbounded `\d+` can raise on subprocess output. The hole was found and fixed **once** in `cyanrip_log.py` on 2026-07-28 — and the fix *and its pinned test* were scoped to that module, leaving six identical holes in parsers that documented the opposite. §5.o verbatim. It also found `_goal_driven_widgets()` called from nowhere *and* listing five of six controls, omitting the exact one the code beside it records as a shipped bug.

  **The validation pass found a traversal and a silent reset.** `safe_path_segment` refuses `.`/`..` and was wired to the unknown-album path only, so an album titled `..` escaped the output dir on the ordinary known-disc path — the same shape as the `%Y` escape fixed 2026-07-28 and, again, closed only where it was learned. And `read_offset = 99999` with the override on loaded as **0 with the override still true**, which rips the next disc at the wrong offset; the hazard was already *written down* in a docstring and closed on the write path only. Also `--ctdb-calibrate ./-x` → `-x/track.flac` argv entries that `flac` parses as options.

  **Performance, measured, and the honest answer was mostly "nothing here".** Startup is 295 ms to a shown window against a 3.45 s cold-container probe (imports are 8% of perceived readiness); the per-line rip loop costs ~17 µs/line against a rip measured in tens of minutes. The real find was a **bug**: a quadratic regex — 3.9 s on one 2000-character row — parsing a user-edited CSV **on the GUI thread before the window is shown**. Three more super-linear patterns were bounded, and the durable output is `tests/test_regex_bounded_time.py`, which flagged two of the four independently on its first run. The one genuine user-facing win (serial per-track recompress/transcode/verify on a ≥4-core box) was **deliberately left alone**: `flac`/`ffmpeg` are absent in CI so the base is unmeasured, and guessing would have been inventing a problem.

  **Two corrections to my own reporting, both worth recording.** I said the rip mixin sat at 19% branch coverage; measured cleanly it was ~81%, now 97% — the 19% came from a run with two failures against a concurrently-edited tree, and the figures weren't even self-consistent. And I said EAC lists pregaps for fourteen tracks; it is **ten of fourteen**, because the committed baseline is two concatenated EAC runs and a whole-file count doubles.

  - **Lessons (durable):** (1) ***A guard is code, and needs the same scepticism as code.*** `test_security_no_shell` enumerated the whole tree and had **no floor** — renaming the package would have turned it green while reading zero files. The project's own "can this be satisfied by finding nothing?" question had never been asked *of the guards*. Graduated: every structural sweep now carries an "examined ≥ N" floor. (2) ***A revert proof can lie if the bytecode cache is warm*** — a restore-then-revert inside the same second produced an identical file size and CPython reused a stale `.pyc`, making a real fix look vacuous. Re-run with `PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1`. (3) ***A number carried from a summary instead of re-derived from the source is not evidence*** — twice in one session, and both times an agent re-deriving it caught me. (4) ***When a fix is scoped to the module where the bug was found, assume the other modules have it.*** Three separate findings this session were the same defect surviving in files the original fix never visited.

- **The first hardware batch came back and the release that claimed a fix had not shipped one (2026-07-30, continued session).** The maintainer began returning results piece by piece — A10's run, then the four artifacts (`.cue`, rip log, EAC log, `.platterpus.json`). Four outcomes, and only one of them was the test I had asked for.

  **A10 passed.** 16-track CD-R, tracks 1–2 deselected: the `.cue` pairs every typed title with its own track number (`TRACK 03` → `"three3"`, `TRACK 05` → `"five5"` / `PERFORMER "if madrid - ft. nelly"`), and Picard reads the same back out of the tags. Under the v0.5.18 bug `TRACK 03` would have carried row 1's title. The off-by-one is gone on real hardware. Their `metaflac` loop errored for an unrelated reason — the `cd` had an unquoted path with a space, so the glob ran in `$HOME` — and the sheet now carries a `find`-based one-liner with no `cd` at all.

  **A19 failed, and it was my fix that failed.** v0.5.18's changelog claims the EAC log's `Gap handling` row now says "Appended to previous track". The rig's log said `None signalled`. The fix was written as `info.gap_detection or ("Appended…" if cyanrip else …)` — reachable only when cyanrip reports *nothing*, and cyanrip always prints its `Gaps:` block. So the row shipped unchanged and I had told the maintainer to expect the new wording. **Its test could not have caught it: the fixture set `gap_detection` to EAC's phrase — a string cyanrip never emits — so the renderer was handed its expected output as input.** The row was also a category error independent of the bug: cyanrip reports what the TOC *signalled*, EAC's row states the *policy*, and echoing one into the other meant the row never said anything an EAC log says.

  **D1 and D2 answered themselves out of the same log, both favourably.** D1: their A10 rip *was* a subset rip (`Tracks to rip: 3, 4, …, 16`) and the log carries `Accurip:` plus all three local CRCs per track — so `-l` does not disable the AccurateRip machinery and task #55 takes the cheap path. One narrow gap remains (their disc isn't in the database, so we saw `not found`, never a positive confidence under `-l`) and it is now D1b. D2: cyanrip's header prints `C2 errors: unsupported by drive`, we already parse it, and the row reads `No` — first-party evidence from the tool doing the read, which is exactly the standard the test refused my survey-based attempt for. The drive supplied what the survey couldn't.

  **The best find was incidental.** Diffing the committed real EAC 1.8 log against the cyanrip log *of the same disc in the same drive*: EAC lists `Pre-gap length` for **10 of the 14** tracks, cyanrip signals none. (I first wrote "fourteen" — wrong, because the committed baseline is two concatenated EAC runs and a whole-file count doubles. Caught by an agent re-deriving it from the file rather than taking my number.) **EAC detects pregaps cyanrip does not.** Both append to the previous track, so audio and CRCs are unaffected — the archival *record* is thinner than EAC's. That is the KDD-32 / `INDEX 00` gap, now with a concrete measurement behind it instead of a cue-sheet argument, and the corrected row surfaces it rather than burying it. A test pins the disagreement against the real baseline so nobody later "fixes" it by making our string match EAC's.

  **The maintainer also raised the product's real ceiling**, and it belongs in the record verbatim: *"we need to be able to confidently be able to be the gold standard first burn proof as well. until this this will not be a serious product."* Correct, and precisely stated — AccurateRip and CTDB both answer "do other people's rips agree", so a disc nobody has submitted gets grey and the app has no verdict of its own. What is achievable on one drive is reproducibility plus error accounting (N reads agreeing, cache defeat measured, offset confirmed, paranoia clean) — EAC's Test & Copy — and its hard boundary is that **it cannot detect a systematic misread**; only a second drive varies the thing under test. Four candidates recorded in TASKS.md as a new P0 block, unstarted, because the load-bearing one (make Test & Copy the default when AccurateRip has nothing, doubling rip time on unknown discs) is a defaults decision that is the maintainer's to make.

  - **Lessons (durable):** (1) ***A fixture that supplies the expected output as input tests nothing*** — the fourth instance this week of §5.t, and the sharpest: the fixture didn't merely differ from reality, it contained the answer. When a renderer translates A into B, the fixture must hold A; if it holds B the test passes on an empty function. (2) ***An `or` fallback silently narrows a fix to the case where the input is missing.*** `x or new_behaviour` reads like "use the new behaviour" and means "use it only when x is empty" — for a field the upstream tool always populates, that is dead code wearing a fix's name. Check what the real tool actually emits before choosing the guard. (3) ***A hand-authored reference is not evidence of an external standard*** — `tests/fixtures/rip_log_eac_reference.log` is ours, so it cannot confirm EAC's vocabulary; the real committed EAC log and a public one could, and did. (4) ***Telling the tester what to expect turns a silent bug into a reported one.*** A19 was found because the sheet stated an expectation precisely enough to be contradicted; a vaguer instruction would have produced a shrug.

- **The run sheet was scoped to one release; three had landed (2026-07-30, continued session).** Asked for "all the tests needed, not just the most recent" as a Markdown file. That request exposed a real gap in how the sheet was maintained: it was retitled and rewritten each release around *that* release's fixes, on the implicit assumption that the rig runs once per release. It doesn't — v0.5.16, v0.5.17 and v0.5.18 all shipped between rig sessions, so the older unproven items had to be reconstructed from `session-log.md` by hand before a run could even start, which is exactly the kind of reconstruction a checklist exists to prevent.

  Rewrote `docs/hardware-test-checklist.md` as the consolidated sheet, grouped by **why a test is still open** rather than by which release introduced it: §A this release's fixes, §B shipped-but-never-proven from earlier releases, §C never exercised on hardware at all, §D by-hand probes that answer an open design question, §E the cyanrip-`master` build last because it swaps the ripper binary. Ten new §A items (A10–A19) cover what only hardware can prove about v0.5.17/v0.5.18 — tags landing on the right files when tracks are deselected, cancel-then-quit inside five seconds, a failed disc read finally saying *why*. **Test IDs are stable across releases and retired when they pass**, so the numbering gaps are the record of what the rig has already settled and the sheet shrinks over time instead of being rebuilt.

  Two things went in that aren't tests. §A19 lists the **log-wording changes that are intentional**, because the maintainer reads a changed line as a regression by default and that instinct is correct often enough to be worth protecting. And §A17 names the **one behaviour change this release that could plausibly break a working setup** — any non-zero cyanrip `-I` exit is now a hard failure instead of an empty `DiscInfo`, which is the fix, but it means a drive that used to limp along on a warning will now refuse. A checklist that only lists hoped-for outcomes is a worse instrument than one that says where its own change is risky.

  - **Lessons (durable):** (1) ***A per-release artefact silently assumes a per-release cadence.*** The sheet wasn't wrong for v0.5.18; it was wrong about how often the rig runs, and nothing in it recorded that assumption. Where a document's structure encodes a cadence the project doesn't actually keep, the structure is the defect — graduated into `docs/README.md`'s index row, which now states the sheet's scope and the stable-ID/retirement convention so the next session can't quietly re-narrow it. (2) ***Tell the tester which changes are deliberate.*** A hardware sheet is read by someone comparing against remembered output; unannotated intentional changes generate false bug reports and burn the scarcest resource in this project, which is rig time.

- **Three parallel audits, and two of the bugs they found were in the code I had just written (v0.5.18, 2026-07-29, continued session).** Asked to keep going on bugs, debugging and typing, with agents approved. Ran three read-only audits (rip path, error-visibility/thread-safety, typing map) plus a web research pass on EAC tracker requirements, and did the fixes myself.

  **The audits caught me twice, within the hour, on the same module.** I built `platterpus.killable` to give `run_capture` a killable child — and (1) its shared slot was cleared unconditionally in the `finally`, so two overlapping runs meant the *loser* deregistered the *winner*, leaving the live child unkillable and `cancel()` a permanent no-op; and (2) the two new `cancel()` methods were **called from nowhere**, because both `stop_thread` call sites omitted the worker argument. I wrote the rule about exactly that yesterday. Worse, the sweep I wrote to enforce it had the same blind spot — it checks a `cancel()` exists and interrupts, never that it is *invoked* — so it now checks invocation too. **The lesson is not "audit more", it is that a guard written from the same mental model as the bug inherits the bug's blind spot.**

  **The worst diagnostic hole in the app**: `_run` discarded cyanrip's exit code *and* stderr, so a non-zero `-I` produced an empty `DiscInfo` — indistinguishable from "MusicBrainz doesn't know this disc". The GUI announced exactly that and offered an unknown-album rip while the real cause (permission denied on the device, a dead container) appeared in **no log line anywhere**. The user is told the wrong thing about the wrong subsystem and their bug report contains no evidence.

  **Two silent data-correctness bugs**, both reported as success. The unknown-disc tagger keyed each track on the file's *position*, which equals the track number only when every track was ripped — and the Rip? column exists so it isn't; untick track 1 and every tag on the archival master is off by one. And `rip_dir` fell back to `params.output_dir` when the log was missing — the output **root** — so every post-rip step walked the whole music library: MP3s derived from every album ever ripped, and a report that hashed them. The library-move step already refused that case, which is what makes the other five an oversight rather than a decision.

  **Typing:** `report_types.py` names the `.platterpus.json` shape as 33 TypedDicts, retiring 4 of 14 ratchet entries. Two facts worth remembering: a TypedDict is **not** assignable to `dict[str, object]` (`Mapping` accepts it), and the `x or {}` idiom breaks `.get()` on a TypedDict — three sites fixed, each a small lie even before the types said so. `warn_unreachable` stays **off**, now with the reasoning recorded: all five hits are false positives and one is a cross-thread cancel checkpoint that mypy narrows away because it reasons single-threaded.

  **EAC / tracker comparison** (the maintainer asked): a cyanrip log will **never** pass a tracker logchecker. The gate is ripper *identity*, checked before a single quality line, so closing every quality gap moves the score from unscoreable to unscoreable. Precise and worth knowing: whipper's *accepted* checksum is a plain SHA-256 of every line but the last — structurally the same scheme as Platterpus's own footer — so the checksum wall is not the wall; identity is. That corrects a claim in our own feasibility doc. Full row-by-row comparison in `docs/eac-parity.md`.

  **A change attempted and reverted, which is the part worth keeping.** The research said libcdio-paranoia never uses C2 error pointers, so I asserted `Make use of C2 pointers : No` — and `test_does_not_fabricate_read_mode_or_c2_pointers` failed. The test was right and I was wrong: the silent-blocks and null-samples rows are asserted because each has *direct evidence*; a secondary source summarising libcdio is not that standard. Reverted, with what would earn it recorded in TASKS.md.

  - **Lessons (durable):** (1) ***A guard inherits the blind spot of whoever wrote the bug*** — my cancel sweep checked existence and interruption but not invocation, the precise gap that let my own dead `cancel()` ship. Graduated into `test_qthread_ownership.py` as a third check. (2) ***Log lazily-formatted objects at your peril*** — the crash handler passed a live `QThread` as a `%s` arg, and `logging` formats later, so once the worker exited, formatting raised inside logging, *from the crash handler*. Log a plain string. (3) ***`sys.excepthook` does not cover `threading.Thread`*** — `threading.excepthook` is separate and its default writes to a stderr an AppImage does not have. (4) ***A fixture with unrealistic names can hide an off-by-one forever*** — `a.flac`/`track1.flac` made position and track number agree by construction (§5.t again, third time this week). (5) ***An external standard is worth measuring against even when you can never satisfy it*** — the tracker requirements are mostly about read correctness, and the comparison turned two of our "unknown" rows into work items and one into a documented refusal.

  **Deliberately not fixed, recorded in TASKS.md with mechanism and scenario:** eleven findings, led by the re-rip keeping the first pass's AccurateRip verdict (a track can read "verified" when the shipped bytes were never checked — the worst of the three passes, and hardware-gated on whether cyanrip emits `Accurip` lines under `-l`), `_auto_force_stop` firing after every cancel and able to eject the *wrong* drive, and stale files in an album folder contaminating the next rip's verification.

- **The build had been green while running 76% of the suite, and four separate "capabilities" existed only in their own docstrings (v0.5.17, 2026-07-29, continued session).** Asked to merge the QThread branch and cut the release. The merge went in on ten green CI checks — and then the release-prep test run produced output that stopped at `[ 75%]` with **no `N passed` summary line**. That absence was the only clue, and it was the whole thing.

  **The green tick was not green.** `conftest` deliberately ends the session with `os._exit(status)` (it dodges a PySide global-teardown abort), and its comment claimed that could not mask a mid-run abort. True for a *signal*; false for a mid-run **`os._exit(0)`**, which is byte-identical to success from outside. Product code supplies exactly that: `hard_exit` exists to leave the process without teardown, and a test driving the update-relaunch path called it. So ~500 tests never ran, `--cov-fail-under` never evaluated, and **a genuinely failing test was swallowed** — reverting the fix showed exit code 0 with an `F` already recorded. CI's log for the merged PR ends at the identical point. I merged on a check that had stopped running, and said so.

  Two aggravating details, both about diagnosability rather than logic: CI passed `-q` on top of the `-q` in `addopts`, taking verbosity to `-2`, which **suppresses the summary line** — so its absence looked normal. And `mypy` on `PATH` was a stale global 1.19.1 against a `>=2.3,<2.4` pin, inventing 118 phantom import errors until I checked `--version`. **Gating tools get `python -m`; a number you did not check the tool version for is not a number.**

  **The fix has to come from outside the run**, because a truncated process cannot report on itself. `pytest_sessionfinish` writes `.pytest-session-complete` as its very last act, `pytest_sessionstart` deletes any stale copy (yesterday's would vouch for today), and CI fails the job when it is missing. The seam that should have prevented the whole thing — `hard_exit._exit_fn`, documented as "reached only when a test injects a recording stub" — **had never once been injected**, so it is now patched from an *autouse* fixture, and the stand-in **raises** rather than returning, because `os._exit` never returns and a stub that falls through lets tests walk a path production cannot reach.

  **Then the batch of audit findings, all reproduced before being fixed.** The rip worker ended with an unbounded `wait()` while the read loop `break`s on cancel — a pipe holds ~64 KiB, the ripper blocks writing, and the wait never returns, on the worker's own thread. `RipHandle.cancel()`, the SIGTERM→SIGKILL escalation, was **called from nowhere in `src/`** while the cancel path's docstring pointed at it; wiring it into the bounded reap fixed both at once. Closing the drive-setup dialog left `cd-paranoia -A` reading the disc for up to 600 s, because `cancel_setup` resolved to the **ABC's concrete no-op** that the cyanrip backend never overrode. The pending-installs dialog had no teardown for its worker thread at all. And the "Set up drive" dialog's `setMinimumSize(460, 320)` was a guess 185 px shorter than its content needs — measured, the intro label was **73 px short at 440×300**.

  - **Lessons (durable, graduated):** (1) ***An exit code is only a verdict if the run reached the end*** → `docs/testing.md` §5.q, with the liveness-artefact pattern for any harness that hard-exits, forks, or `execv`s. (2) ***A documented capability is not a capability*** → §5.p, with the four-row table of docstrings that described code which did not exist; grep for a call site, and check it is **reachable**, not merely present — my own wiring test's first version passed against a reverted tree because the call sat in a helper nothing called any more (the "mentioning is not stopping" trap, hit a *second* time). (3) ***A concrete no-op default on an ABC is a hazard***: the subclass inherits "handled" for free and no abstract-method error ever fires — test that the shipped subclass overrides it, comparing functions, since `hasattr` passes either way. (4) ***Never `wait()` on a child whose pipe you stopped draining***, and bound the post-SIGKILL wait too (a wedged drive ioctl is uninterruptible) → `CLAUDE.md` rule 9. (5) ***A fake that ignores its timeout cannot stand in for a call that blocks*** — `_FakeHandle.wait()` accepted `timeout` and returned anyway, which is why no test could see the deadlock (§5.t again).

  **Deliberately not changed:** `HostSetupWorker.cancel()` is flag-only against steps that can run 1800 s, and that is the *right* call — it is documented as taking effect at the next step boundary, and killing a half-done `dnf install` is worse than waiting. It is a reasoned limitation, not a false promise, and shutdown now abandons its thread safely. Similarly the pending-installs worker cannot be interrupted (the injected installer owns its own subprocess), so the thread is abandoned and the limitation written down rather than hidden behind a `cancel()` that would do nothing.

  **Coda, same session: the maintainer asked "are we good now? proactive solutions should be implemented" — and checking instead of asserting found the answer was *not yet*.** Every rule that had mattered all cycle was written down *and* enforced — in exactly one place. `CLAUDE.md` rule 9 is most of a page on not destroying a live `QThread`; `test_harness_fidelity.py` polices it thoroughly for `MainWindow`. **Nine classes create threads. One was checked.** The dialog with no teardown was found by a person reading code, which is luck, not a control.

  So four sweeps now derive their targets from the source tree rather than a list — a class written next month is covered the day it is written: every `self.x = QThread(...)` owner must be stopped from a **reachable** teardown hook; responsibility must exist at all (the `PendingInstallsDialog` shape); a flag-only `cancel()` must carry a written justification in an allowlist with a staleness check; and the five workers with no `cancel()` at all are a **shrink-only ratchet**. Proven by reverting: deleting that dialog's `closeEvent` fails naming the class and `_install_thread`.

  **The obvious implementation was confidently wrong again.** A file-scoped version flagged `RipMixin`, `DependencyMixin` and `UpdateMixin` — they create threads `MainWindow.closeEvent` stops — and "fixing" that by giving a mixin its own `closeEvent` would have *broken* the concrete class's teardown. Responsibility has to resolve **downward** through the MRO. Third time this cycle that the tidy-looking fix was the trap (after the nested scroll area and the wheel probe).

  Also surfaced by writing it: `UpdateInstallWorker.cancel` is flag-only and was unclassified — before allowlisting it I read `download_and_install` and confirmed the flag is tested at the top of every chunk-read, so the flag genuinely *is* the interrupt. Verified, not assumed, which is the whole difference between an allowlist and an excuse.

  - **Lesson (durable, graduated):** ***enforce a rule across the codebase, not at the place it was learned*** → `docs/testing.md` §5.o. Derive the population from the AST rather than listing it (a list is a memory aid, and memory is what failed); give the sweep a floor so a broken walk fails loudly; make the exception explicit and *reasoned*; and ratchet a gap you are not closing today, the way the mypy per-module opt-outs already work. The test that covers only the instance you just fixed is the cheapest to write and the likeliest to let the same bug back in somewhere else.

  **No release cut for it** — tests and docs only, zero `src/` change, so an AppImage would be functionally identical to v0.5.17. The standing merge-then-release rule exists so user-facing fixes ship promptly; there is nothing user-facing here, and `[Unreleased]` will carry it into the next real release. Left tracked in `TASKS.md`: the `run_capture` killable-child work (its own cycle — it touches the helper every backend probe goes through) and the hardware round with the four things to watch.

- **A coredump named the wrong culprit, the retention list was already in place, and the suite was quietly doing the product's job (v0.5.17, 2026-07-29, continued session).** The maintainer supplied `platterpus-qthread-crash-fix.md` — a real coredump plus journal trace, six proposed fixes, a non-negotiable typing standard, and blanket permission to act. Two of the six fixes were wrong for the actual code, and the mechanism was neither of the ones proposed.

  **Reproduce before you fix — it cost ten minutes and changed the answer.** A child process that abandons a running `QThread` and then exits *normally* dies with **134 (SIGABRT)**, logging the exact line from the crash report. The brief's Fix 2 said the code drops the QThread reference; it does not — the module-level retention list was **already there when it crashed**. The reason it did not help is that **CPython clears module globals during interpreter shutdown**, so the last reference drops at exit anyway. `hard_exit.py` (deliberately Qt-free, so it is unit-testable without a `QApplication`) bypasses teardown when a thread was abandoned, flushing the log first because `os._exit` does not — and a truncated log is exactly what a bug report needs.

  **Two of the brief's fixes were rejected with a measurement, not an opinion.** Fix 4's 10 s per-worker wait multiplies: `closeEvent` stops six workers, so it is a **60 s frozen window** — replaced with one shared monotonic `ShutdownDeadline` (and never hand `QThread.wait()` a negative number: Qt reads it as *wait forever*). Fix 5's `env=os.environ.copy()` on relaunch would reintroduce a bug that `_relaunch_env()` exists to prevent. Saying so beat forcing them.

  **Three parallel read-only audits found more than the brief did, including a defect in my own fix.** `closeEvent` stopped six worker threads and **not the rip thread** — cancelling the rip *worker* and freeing the drive, but `_rip_thread` is parented to the window, so `~QMainWindow` destroyed a live `QThread`. My hard-exit guard had **latched**: one call site abandons a thread unconditionally and the retention list was append-only, so a single mid-probe rescan turned `os._exit` into the *normal* exit for the rest of the session. And cancel-then-quit inside five seconds left the drive reading with **no in-app or hardware recovery** (the physical eject button is ignored while a read holds the device) — the 2026-07-01 real-user bug reachable through a different door. Auditing the fix found as much as auditing the code.

  **Why all of it escaped, in one sentence: a stand-in behaved better than the real thing.** `conftest.stop_window_threads` stopped the rip thread `closeEvent` did not, so the abort was unreachable from every test while being reliably reachable by a user — for five releases. That is a class, not an incident, and it is invisible to ordinary tests by construction: a test cannot notice that its own scaffolding is holding the product up. `tests/test_harness_fidelity.py` checks it from the outside.

  **The new detector passed against the exact bug it was written for.** Its first version collected every `self._rip_thread` in the source; `_stop_rip_on_shutdown` *mentions* the thread in a guard clause without stopping it. Mentioning is not stopping. Caught only by reverting the fix and watching the test stay green — the second vacuous detector in two sessions, which is why "would this fail if I reverted the fix?" is now a question answered in the commit message rather than in someone's head.

  - **Lessons (durable, all graduated):** (1) ***Retaining a reference does not make process exit safe*** → `CLAUDE.md` Critical rule 9, with the whole Qt-threading set (and **"detach" is banned** — Qt has no such operation, and the word made a fatal pattern look supported). (2) ***A stand-in must not be better than the real thing***; ***every detector needs a vacuity floor***; ***prove non-vacuity by reverting the fix*** → `docs/testing.md` §5.t. (3) ***A fix is a change*** — three consecutive releases each fixed a real bug and introduced the next, so ask what new state the fix creates and what tests *that* → §5.s, plus the pre-flight checklist in `CLAUDE.md`. (4) ***A tool that gates CI must not float***, and a number quoted from a tool carries the tool's version → §5.r; `ruff` and `mypy` are pinned to their measured minors. (5) ***Typing is a ratchet, not a flag*** → Critical rule 10; `disallow_any_generics` alone was 131 of 132 errors, and the opt-out block carries measured per-module counts so it can only shrink.

  **Reported, not fixed — deliberately.** The audits surfaced more than one release should absorb. The largest still open: `RipWorker._handle.wait()` has **no timeout on an undrained `PIPE`** (a deadlock), and `RipHandle.cancel()` (SIGTERM→SIGKILL) is **never called anywhere in `src/`**. Several `cancel()` implementations are flag-only against steps that block for 600–1800 s, which makes them false promises rather than slow ones. Those are named in full rather than quietly carried.

- **The scroll-area fix worked, and its own side effect became the next bug (v0.5.16, 2026-07-29, continued session).** The maintainer confirmed v0.5.15: *"All works, but the 2 scroll bars in the lower right are difficult to use together; make something better."* Overlap gone, new complaint — and a fair one, because the fix caused it. A `QTableWidget` and a `QPlainTextEdit` **are** scroll areas, so putting the whole pane inside one made them *nested*: measured, two vertical scrollbars 15 px apart (x=911 and x=926 on a 940×400 pane) with the wheel acting on whichever the pointer was over.

  **The obvious repair is a trap, and measuring it is what saved the release.** Size the table to its content and set `ScrollBarAlwaysOff` → one scrollbar at every size, 0 at 1900×980.
